Jensen Huang Resigns as Nvidia CEO

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Jensen Huang stepped down as the chief executive of Nvidia on Thursday, four days after the company announced its internal investigation into an affair he had with Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, an alleged asset of China’s Ministry of State Security.

“Jensen Huang has resigned as President, Chief Executive Officer, and a member of the Board, effective immediately,” the Nvidia board said in a statement. Nvidia’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, will serve as interim CEO. The board said it “will consider internal and external candidates” for a permanent replacement.

The board’s investigation was announced on November 8, slightly less than two weeks after The New York Times reported that the chief executive had been the target of a Chinese intelligence operation facilitated through his relationship with Alice Huang, who has no known relation to Jensen. The pair have been spotted in public together at least twice, in June 2024 and May 2025. On November 11, The New York Times reported that Jensen Huang also allegedly had a relationship with a direct report at Nvidia.

The alleged relationship with a subordinate, along with Huang’s business dealings with Megaspeed, a Singapore-based AI company where Alice Huang previously held a senior position, likely played a greater role in the board’s decision to oust Huang than the Chinese espionage allegations, a source familiar with the matter tells WIRED.

US intelligence officials reportedly briefed President Donald Trump on Haung’s relationship with Alice last year, but the president did not act on it. On November 6, The New York Times and Financial Times reported that Alice was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, and that she had been recruited to collect information on the Nvidia executive. (The Chinese government has rejected all claims that Alice was a spy for the country.)

Huang sent a companywide email on Sunday acknowledging his affair, apologizing to his family and Nvidia employees, and pledging cooperation with the internal investigation. The executive has been married to his wife Lori Huang since 1985, and both of the couple’s two children work at the company.

On Monday, WIRED exclusively reported that last year Huang began working with a crisis PR firm in order to manage his personal communications. In October 2025, those PR consultants started to be looped in to all press inquiries involving Huang, whether those inquiries were personal or professional in nature.

The timing lines up with a New York Times report that month that the US Commerce Department was investigating whether Alice’s Megaspeed was helping Chinese companies get around American export controls with the help of a vast web of subsidiaries. The investigation was reportedly halted and transferred to the Justice Department after Nvidia general counsel Tim Teter inquired about it.

Huang has been a vocal critic of these export controls, which he argues unnecessarily constrain the market for American chips, create discrete Chinese and American tech ecosystems, and spur China to build competitive chips of their own. Huang has worked to develop a relationship with Trump in order to advocate for policies that are advantageous for Nvidia, including the idea of opening its markets to China.

Huang cofounded Nvidia in 1993 with friends Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem. The trio reportedly brainstormed their vision for the company in a Denny’s diner, and aspired to bring realistic 3D graphics to personal computers. For the first few decades of its existence, Nvidia was primarily known in the gaming community for creating chips powerful enough to render high-quality in-game graphics.

In 2012, after a new kind of neural network known as AlexNet won a high-profile prize in computer vision, Nvidia pivoted from high-speed computer graphics to building chips that would support artificial intelligence. Huang has repeatedly said that the introduction of AlexNet, which was developed by AI engineers Alex Krizhevsky, Ilya Sutskever, and Geoffrey Hinton, was a “Big Bang”-like event for computing,

Because its new GPUs proved highly effective at powering AI models that require massive amounts of processing power, the value of Nvidia shares soared in 2023 and 2024, in lockstep with the broader AI boom that began sweeping through the world economy. In the summer of 2025, Nvidia became the first company in history to cross $4 trillion in value.

As of September 2026, the company controlled about 90 percent of the GPU market. Much of Nvidia’s epic rise over the few decades has been attributed not just to its cutting edge chips and its software “moat,” CUDA, that keeps developers locked into those chips; but also to the fast-thinking, controlling, charismatic, and often bombastic leadership style of Huang himself. Now, that critical part of the company’s success is out.

Who will replace Pierre Sage at Crystal Palace? Odds suspended on Thomas Frank

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The former Tottenham and Brentford boss is the favourite to take over the Eagles

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Pierre Sage became the second managerial casualty of the Premier League season as the former Crystal Palace manager was sacked mere hours after Manchester United had removed Michael Carrick from his position.

His departure is unsurprising given Palace’s position at the bottom of the table and the Frenchman has left the club following a fourth successive defeat.

Sage made the switch to Selhurst Park in the summer to replace Oliver Glasner - who has since moved to Nottingham Forest - and he signed a three-year contract.

He lasted just 16 games in all competitions at Palace and claimed just seven points out of a possible 30 during his tenure,

The search is now on for his replacement ahead of Palace’s next fixture on Saturday 21 November where they will come up against Frank Lampard’s Coventry City.

The current, overwhelming favourite to take up the head coach role is former Tottenham and Brentford boss Thomas Frank with betting sites having suspended betting on him. Frank was the 1/4 favourite to replace Sage before the axe fell on the former Eagles boss and his appointment at Selhurst Park would end an eight month exile from the top-flight after Frank was sacked by Spurs back in February.

Frank has been out of work since his early departure from Spurs but his previous role at Brentford is proof of his credentials and a possible snapshot of what he could achieve with Palace.

However, in taking on the role Frank will firstly need to deal with a relegation battle and ensure the club remains in the Premier League. Palace sold several key players last summer including Maxence Lacroix, Brennan Johnson, and Daniel Munoz while star men Jean-Philippe Mateta and Ismaila Sarr have been struck with injuries.

Palace are yet to make a decision on their next manager, though all signs point to Frank, but former Burnley and Everton manager Sean Dyche is also in the running with betting sites giving him odds of 6/1 to replace Sage.

Dyche certainly has pedigree when it comes to avoiding relegation having kept Burnley in the top-flight from 2016 until his sacking in 2022 and avoiding the drop with Everton in 2023. Dyche would perhaps be a short term option for the Eagles but he would also be a future-proof one having twice lifted Burnley out of the Championship and into the Premier League.

Danish manager Jens Berthel Askou has reportedly emerged as a candidate for the Crystal Palace job with bookmakers offering a 6/1 chance for him to replace Sage as well.

After a season in charge of Motherwell, Askou moved to Toulouse and has since made a steady start to life in French football, with his team sitting 12th in Ligue 1 after collecting 13 points from their opening 10 matches.

His side have recorded three victories, four draws and three defeats, scoring 15 goals and conceding 14.

Askou has caught the eye of Crystal Palace’s decision makers but his appointment would be a riskier option considering managers are available with previous Premier League experience.

Other possible choices are Tony Popovic at 7/1, while Vitor Pereira and Vitor Matos both sit at 12/1 on betting sites.

Jensen Huang Resigns From Nvidia

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Jensen Huang founded Nvidia back in 1993 and called each of the company’s major shots along the way, from its focus on gaming graphics to its latest pivot to AI. He’s now resigned as CEO following reports of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

“Board of Directors announced today that Jensen Huang has resigned as President, Chief Executive Officer, and a member of the Board, effective immediately,” reads a new press release from the tech giant on November 12. Colette Kress, Nvidia’s current chief financial officer, has been named as interim CEO to replace Huang while the board of directors initiates a search for a permanent replacement. Investor Harvey Jones has been appointed chair of the board following Huang’s departure.

“The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the companyâs operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption,” Jones said in a statement. Kress said she’ll build on the “extraordinary” foundation left by Huang and focus on serving the company’s customers.

“Jensenâs contributions to Nvidia are profound,” the press release stated. “Nvidia is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. The Board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry.”

Huang’s resignation comes after a report in The New York Times of a second extramarital affair, this time with Nvidia’s VP of healthcare technology. Earlier in the week, the 63-year-old executive admitted to a separate romantic relationship with an alleged Chinese spy. Nvidia’s stock price has shed hundreds of billions in the intervening days, and the company faces at least one lawsuit from shareholders.

Once a humble graphics card company lobbying video game console makers for contracts to supply them with chips, Nvidia has become the epicenter for the current boom in AI tech, rising to a multi-trillion-dollar market cap in just a couple of years as it shifted from servicing the PC gaming market to feeding an insatiable demand for new data center buildout.

The Argentine is set to become the fourth permanent head coach of the INEOS era after Manchester United decided to dismiss Michael Carrick, with the former Red Devils midfielder having won just once in the first 10 games of the season.

Pochettino has already decided on a first transfer target, too – with Arsenal's Hale End academy another source for the club.

Two of United's early INEOS buys came from the Gunners, with the new ownership looking for value in youngsters they could develop.

Ayden Heaven has proven to be a smart signing, while 18-year-old Chido Obi is still developing out on loan at Willem II.

Tesla to keep producing Cybertrucks until the end of next summer

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The Cybertruck saga continues. Days after Tesla CEO Elon Musk said the company would wind down production of its electric pickup, he's backtracking that decision, but not by much.

Last week, Musk said Tesla will stop producing Cybertrucks at Gigafactory Texas "around the middle of next year." Given that it's the only factory where Cybertrucks are manufactured, that meant the end of the road for the Cybertruck, with the company using the production capacity for its autonomous taxi car, the Cybercab.

Musk quickly followed up by saying that the demand for Cybertruck is strong and that the order rate "far exceeds" the company's production rate. "We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months," he wrote on X.

Counting from the day he said it (early November 2026), that would mean ending production in early June 2027.

Now, however, Musk says Tesla will extend that deadline by a couple of months. The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

"In order to work through more of the Cybertruck order backlog, production will be extended through the end of summer 2027," he wrote on X.

"This is not the smartest move from a profit-maximizing standpoint btw, but there must be some room in this world for sentimentality or what’s the point," he added.

It's odd to call the move "working through the backlog," as Cybertruck can still be ordered on Tesla's U.S. website, though only in select configurations. Moreover, the company actually raised prices of the Cybertruck on the day Musk announced it would be winding down production.

Still, this should mean that more people who have already ordered the Cybertruck will actually get their car. That's a good thing, right?

Bit Digital stock falls 5% in pre-market after Q3 revenue miss

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Bit Digital’s $27 million third-quarter revenue came in 16% below analyst consensus estimates, marking an over $5 million shortfall.

Shares in Nasdaq-listed Bit Digital fell 9.3% in premarket trading on Thursday after the Ethereum treasury and AI infrastructure company reported third-quarter revenue below analysts’ estimates.

Bit Digital reported $27.1 million in revenue, down 11% from $30.5 million a year earlier. Net income attributable to shareholders was $28.5 million, or 8 cents per diluted share, according to its Thursday SEC filing.

The company’s revenue came in nearly 16% below analyst consensus estimates of about $32.2 million, marking a $5.1 million shortfall, according to Yahoo Finance.

Bit Digital reported $1 million in Ether (ETH) staking revenue, down about 66% from $2.9 million a year ago. The company reported holdings equivalent to about 163,726 ETH, worth $439.4 million as of Sept. 30.

Its Bitcoin mining revenue fell to $2 million from $7.4 million a year earlier.

Bit Digital said that the decline in mining revenue was “consistent with the continued winddown of the business.” The company revealed it pivoted its corporate treasury strategy from Bitcoin to Ether in July 2025.

Anthony Clark, Yes, Dear Star and Comedian, Dead at 62

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Anthony Clark, a comedian known for his roles in Yes, Dear and Boston Common, has died, his former castmate Mike O’Malley confirmed. He was 62.

Anthony Clark, a comedian who starred in Yes, Dear and Boston Common, died Nov. 10, his former castmate Mike O’Malley confirmed in a post on social media. He was 62.

“He was a hilarious guy,” O’Malley wrote in a tribute shared to Instagram Nov. 12. “He was so unique—one of those comics who is laughing along with you at the absurdity of his stories and observations and it just made you laugh harder because he was laughing. I was a fan before I was a friend. We spoke on the phone three weeks ago. He had us laughing loudly as he always did.”

O’Malley—who starred alongside Clark on Yes, Dear—included photos from their last day of set, nodding to the sentimental value.

“Attached are some snapshots from our last episode of ‘Yes Dear’ which ended twenty years ago,” he continued. “He invested his money well and essentially retired after we wrapped. Rest in peace, Tony.”

Hailing from Virginia and attending Emerson College, Clark made his television debut on American Playhouse in 1991. He went on to score roles in Ellen DeGeneres’ sitcom Ellen and the romantic comedy The Thing Called Love before booking the role of Boyd Pritchett on Boston Common in 1996.

From there, he secured his role as studio executive Greg Warner in Yes, Dear for 122 episodes alongside O’Malley, as well as Jean Louisa Kelly and Liza Snyder. Having also established a career in stand-up in tandem with his acting, Clark hosted the fourth season of The Last Comic Standing in 2006.

Following his performance in Yes, Dear, Clark made a few additional minor appearances in films before retreating from the spotlight entirely.

Still, those who did get to know him would agree with how his agent Barry Katz put it in his own tribute, noting that Clark was “arguably one of the funniest people that I have ever met in my lifetime.”

Ex-Seattle startup DeltaGen acquired by new AI company backed by Veeam co-founder

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Chronicling the Seattle and Pacific Northwest startup scene. SEE MORE

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 12, 2026 at 5:51 amNovember 12, 2026 at 5:51 am

DeltaGen, an AI startup that got its start in Seattle before moving to San Francisco this year, has been acquired by Worker.ai, a new small-business software company backed by Veeam founder Ratmir Timashev.

Worker, which launched Thursday, acquired all of DeltaGen for an undisclosed price that included a substantial cash component and equity in Worker, DeltaGen co-founder and CEO Rene Bystron told GeekWire.

Bystron joins Worker as co-founder and COO, and nine other DeltaGen employees are coming with him, including co-founders Michal Studnicka and Matus Ondreicka.

Worker, led by co-founder and CEO John Roberts, a former private equity operator based in Raleigh, N.C., is building software that lets small businesses hand off recurring work to AI agents. DeltaGen’s voice technology captures decisions made in conversations and turns them into assigned tasks.

Roberts had been a DeltaGen customer before Worker needed voice technology, Bystron said. “We could have raised another round and kept building independently, but this gave us the capital and experience to move faster with people we trusted,” he said.

Launched in 2024, DeltaGen raised a $1.2 million pre-seed round in early 2025 to build generative AI tools for financial professionals, with a focus on streamlining mergers and acquisitions. The company relocated from Seattle to San Francisco earlier this year.

“I still love Seattle and miss it like crazy,” said Bystron, who is keeping his apartment in Belltown. “But being closer to the AI founders, customers and investors in San Francisco makes a real difference at this stage. It also didn’t help that Washington has been chipping away at its tax advantage.”

Timashev, who co-founded data backup giant Veeam, is backing Worker to help small businesses such as manufacturers, service companies and family businesses grow.

“AI can now give small businesses more capacity without asking already stretched owners to work longer days,” he said in a statement. “Worker is my bet on that shift.” The size of his investment was not disclosed.

DeltaGen’s current product is an AI voice platform, which will continue as it’s integrated into Worker. DeltaGen’s AI voice platform will continue as it’s integrated into Worker, which is opening access gradually through a waitlist.

Disney just launched 3D movies on the Meta Quest ahead of Meta VR Glasses’ release

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

All available 3D movies are included with your Disney+ subscription and come with extras you can only experience in VR.

While 3D movies on a VR headset are uniquely amazing (much more convincing than 3D TV ever was), the most exciting part of the Meta Quest experience is the immersive environments. Disney and Meta worked together to create a fully unique movie-watching experience that gives movies more than just the depth that 3D movies typically have; you’ll also be watching the movies in an environment that’s unique and changes throughout the movie.

Meta says you can “Catch Star Wars: A New Hope (Episode IV) from a comfy spot on Tatooine, see the Avengers battle Loki from atop Avengers Tower, settle in for a Pixar movie night on the Monsters, Inc. Scare Floor, and more.” Some of these immersive environments were shown off at Meta Connect in September when Meta VR Glasses were announced, but it’s a bit surprising to see so many of them launch months ahead of VR Glasses’ retail debut.

Disney also highlights IMAX experiences, which are also available in the Disney+ app on Meta Quest headsets. These movies mimic the full IMAX experience in VR by putting you in a virtual theater with a massive screen, all at the correct IMAX aspect ratio. Rumor also has it that Meta will include other 3D movies with its Meta VR+ subscription service.

Dancing may shape how the brain responds to other people’s emotions

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Ballet dancers show different brain responses to emotional faces, without identifying emotions more accurately than people without training.

Ballet dancers spend years training their bodies to move with precision.

A new study suggests that the same training is also linked to how the brain responds when a dancer sees fear or happiness on someone’s face.

When ballet dancers looked at photos of fearful, happy, and neutral faces, their brain activity differed from that of people with no dance training.

The differences appeared in brain areas that process vision and in areas that process touch.

Beatriz Calvo-Merino worked with lead author Vasiliki Meletaki and Bettina Forster at City St George’s, University of London’s Centre for Cognition and Neuroscience.

“The impact of dance expertise goes beyond movement and taps into social perception mechanisms, such as face processing and emotions,” said Calvo-Merino.

The team compared 26 professional or trainee ballet dancers with 26 people of similar age and sex who had no dance experience.

The dancers had been dancing for about 17 years on average.

Both groups judged the emotions about equally well, but their brain signals differed in timing and pattern.

Earlier brain-scanning studies, including work by Calvo-Merino, found increased activity in the brains of expert dancers when they watched familiar dance movements.

Nearly all of that research looked at how dancers perceive movement and bodies, the authors wrote.

The team used faces to test whether dancers’ brains also differ in other, everyday kinds of perception.

The test is based on an idea about emotion.

According to this idea, people perceive someone else’s emotion partly through their own body’s sense of that emotion, and not through vision alone.

Researchers describe this as an embodied view of emotion.

Earlier experiments found that the brain area receiving touch and movement signals from the body is involved when people look at emotional faces. That area is the somatosensory cortex.

Professional dancers develop a refined awareness of their own bodies through long, precise training, the authors wrote.

Dance training might also refine the body’s part in perceiving emotion, the team reasoned. If so, dancers’ brains should respond differently to emotional faces as well as to dance.

Each volunteer wore a cap fitted with 64 electrodes. The electrodes recorded the brain’s electrical activity, a method called EEG.

The faces came from a photo collection called the Karolinska Directed Emotional Faces. The team used 60 black-and-white photos, half of them male faces.

Each face stayed on screen for 600 milliseconds, a little over half a second.

In one task, a question about emotion sometimes followed a face, such as “Is s/he fearful?” In the other task, used as a comparison, the question asked whether the face was male or female.

Half of the time, a blunt-tipped device gave a brief touch to the tip of the volunteer’s left index finger.

The touch came 105 milliseconds after a face appeared, and volunteers were told to ignore it.

The touch served as a probe. The team recorded the brain’s response to a face with a touch and to a face alone, then subtracted the second recording from the first.

The subtraction helped isolate the brain’s response to touch. Differences between facial expressions could then indicate that emotional processing was influencing that response.

In the dancers, the touch response differed between fearful and neutral faces at the earliest stage the team analyzed.

That stage was about 143 to 173 milliseconds after the face appeared, less than a fifth of a second.

The response to happy faces didn’t differ from the response to neutral faces at that stage. The early effect was specific to fearful expressions, the authors wrote.

In non-dancers, the touch response didn’t differ by emotion at that first stage.

Their touch responses did differ by emotion at later stages, but at a narrower set of recording sites than in dancers.

“What is particularly interesting is that dance expertise appears to influence how the somatosensory cortex responds to emotions expressed by others,” Forster said.

“This suggests that our own bodily experiences can shape how the brain processes other people’s emotions, providing further evidence for the embodied nature of emotion perception.”

Electrodes over the visual areas at the back of the head recorded a second set of differences.

One difference appeared between about 150 and 200 milliseconds after a face came on screen. In dancers, visual responses to neutral faces differed from responses to both fearful and happy faces.

In non-dancers, visual responses at that stage were different for each of the three expressions. Those differences were also stronger on the right side of the brain, a pattern that earlier face studies have typically reported.

In dancers, the differences were spread across both sides. The authors called the two patterns distinct styles of telling emotions apart.

Both groups processed fearful faces in a distinct way. The authors linked that result to the priority the brain is known to give fear.

The authors also wrote, as an anecdote, that fear is an important tool in dance training. Dancers learn to feel comfortable performing in front of an audience, for example.

The study found no clear advantage in dancers’ ability to identify facial emotions.

During the recordings, dancers answered 94% of the emotion questions correctly. Non-dancers answered 95% of them correctly, a gap too small to rule out chance.

Dancers did score higher on a questionnaire about awareness of signals from inside the body. Their average score was 3.69, compared with 2.71 for non-dancers.

Body awareness may be part of how dancers process other people’s emotions, the authors suggested. They proposed exploring that relationship further.

Each group had 26 people, a size chosen to match earlier studies. The authors sugg that future studies should calculate in advance how many participants they need.

Scalp recordings also couldn’t show precisely where in the brain the differing touch responses arose.

The study compared the two groups at a single point in time, so it can’t show how the differences developed.

The next question is whether the brain differences are accompanied by differences in behavior.

Future studies could add behavioral tests of emotion recognition, the authors wrote.

New studies could also explore how the touch area’s response to emotion changes across stages of dance training, from advanced trainees to professional performers.

The full study was published in the journal Cerebral Cortex.

Man Utd managers since Ferguson: Every manager's record, win percentage and time in charge

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

By Calum Burrowes | 12 Nov 2026 13:48, Last updated: 12 Nov 2026 14:05

Manchester United are expected to confirm Mauricio Pochettino as their new head coach following the dismissal of Michael Carrick, who has been relieved of his duties at Old Trafford.

The former Southampton and Tottenham Hotspur boss is expected to leave his role with the United States to take charge of the 20-time English champions, with another managerial change set to begin a new chapter at the club.

Since Alex Ferguson's retirement in 2013, United have appointed a succession of permanent managers and interim coaches, with varying degrees of success.

Here, Sports Mole looks at every manager to take charge of the Red Devils since Ferguson's departure, examining their time in charge, overall record and win percentage.

David Moyes was selected to succeed Ferguson in July 2013 after impressing during his time at Everton, but his spell at Old Trafford proved considerably more difficult than anticipated.

The Scot won the Community Shield in his first competitive fixture, beating Championship side Wigan Athletic 2-0, but United struggled for consistency throughout the season.

Moyes was dismissed in April 2014 after the club's hopes of qualifying for the Champions League had faded, with United eventually finishing seventh in the Premier League.

Now 63, the Scottish manager is back at Everton for a second spell on Merseyside, having returned to the club in January 2025.

Record: 51 matches, 27 wins, 9 draws, 15 defeats

Following Moyes's departure, former United midfielder Ryan Giggs took temporary charge for the final four matches of the 2013-14 season.

Giggs won two, drew one and lost one of those fixtures, including a 4-0 victory over Norwich City.

His brief spell gave him a 50% win percentage, but he was not retained as the club's permanent manager.

Record: 4 matches, 2 wins, 1 draw, 1 defeat

Louis van Gaal arrived in the summer of 2014 after guiding the Netherlands to the World Cup semi-finals in Brazil, bringing considerable managerial experience to Old Trafford.

The Dutchman won 54 of his 103 matches in charge and secured the FA Cup in his second season, beating Crystal Palace in the final.

However, United's failure to qualify for the Champions League proved costly, and Van Gaal was dismissed in May 2016, just two days after lifting the trophy.

Record: 103 matches, 54 wins, 25 draws, 24 defeats

"The Special One" became United's third permanent manager since Ferguson in May 2016, going on to record the highest win percentage of any permanent appointment since the legendary Scot's departure, with 84 victories from 144 matches at a rate of 58.3%.

The Portuguese enjoyed a successful first season, winning the Community Shield, League Cup and Europa League, before guiding the Red Devils to second place in the Premier League in 2017-18.

However, results and performances deteriorated during his third campaign, and he was dismissed in December 2018.

Record: 144 matches, 84 wins, 32 draws, 28 defeats

Former United striker Ole Gunnar Solskjaer initially arrived as caretaker boss in December 2018, enjoying an impressive run of results before being handed the job permanently.

Across 168 matches, Solskjaer recorded 91 wins, 37 draws and 40 defeats, giving him a win percentage of 54.2% and cementing his status as one of the most popular managers among United fans since Ferguson's departure.

United reached the Europa League final  in 2021 and finished second in the Premier League that season, but a poor run of form during the following campaign led to his departure in November 2021.

Record: 168 matches, 91 wins, 37 draws, 40 defeats

Carrick took temporary charge following the Norwegian's dismissal, overseeing three matches between November and December 2022.

The former Tottenham and England midfielder won two and drew one, leaving him unbeaten during his first brief spell and with a win rate of 66.7%.

He subsequently left the club's coaching staff following the appointment of Ralf Rangnick and went on to manage Middlesbrough before returning to Old Trafford earlier this year.

Record: 3 matches, 2 wins, 1 draw, 0 defeats

Ralf Rangnick was appointed interim manager in December 2021 and was expected to oversee a transition before moving into a consultancy role at the end of the season.

However, his time in charge proved difficult, with United winning just 11 of their 29 matches, drawing 10 and losing eight.

His 37.9% win percentage was the lowest of any manager to take charge of the club since Ferguson at that point.

Ralf Rangnick has since spent over four years in charge of Austria, guiding the national team to the 2026 FIFA World Cup.

Record: 29 matches, 11 wins, 10 draws, 8 defeats

Erik ten Hag arrived in May 2022 and made an encouraging start to life in Manchester, winning the League Cup in his first season and restoring the Red Devils to the Champions League.

The Dutchman recorded 70 victories in 128 matches, giving him a win percentage of 54.7%, and added the FA Cup to his trophy collection in 2024.

Nevertheless, inconsistent league performances and a poor start to the 2024-25 campaign resulted in his dismissal in October 2024.

Record: 128 matches, 70 wins, 23 draws, 35 defeats

Van Nistelrooy stepped in as caretaker boss following Ten Hag's departure, having previously served as part of the Dutchman's coaching staff.

The former United striker made an immediate impression, winning three and drawing one of his four matches in charge.

His 75% win percentage is the highest on the list, although his spell was considerably shorter than those of the club's permanent appointments.

Since leaving the Red Devils, Van Nistelrooy has gone on to manage Leicester City, but was dismissed after winning just five of his 27 matches in charge of the Foxes.

Record: 4 matches, 3 wins, 1 draw, 0 defeats

Amorim was appointed in November 2024 after impressing at Sporting Lisbon, but his time at Old Trafford proved challenging despite arriving with a strong reputation.

The Portuguese won 24 of his 63 matches, recording a win rate of 38.1%, and struggled to establish consistency during his time in charge.

His spell ended in January 2026, with United having failed to make the progress expected.

Record: 63 matches, 24 wins, 18 draws, 21 defeats

Former United midfielder Darren Fletcher was placed in temporary charge following Amorim's departure in January 2026.

Fletcher oversaw two matches, drawing one and losing the other, before the club turned to Carrick.

His short spell therefore ended without a victory.

Carrick returned to Old Trafford in January 2026, initially taking charge until the end of the season after United dismissed Amorim, who is currently in charge of AC Milan.

His second spell began impressively, with 12 wins from his first 17 matches helping the Red Devils secure a third-place finish in the Premier League and a return to Champions League football.

Carrick subsequently signed a new contract in May, but his fortunes changed dramatically during the 2026-27 campaign.

United struggled for consistency and found themselves in the relegation zone after 10 league matches, with their dramatic 4-4 draw against Aston Villa proving to be Carrick's final fixture.

The club confirmed his departure on November 12, bringing his second spell to an end less than six months after he signed a new deal with the club, leaving United 18th in the league.

Record: 32 matches, 15 wins, 8 draws, 9 defeats

Best Classes To Play Solo In World Of Warcraft: Forever

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Discover the best classes for solo play in World of Warcraft: Forever—why Hunters and Warlocks shine.

You won't always have someone available to quest with in World of Warcraft: Forever, so it's worth picking a class that can hold its own. While every class can reach level 60 solo, some make the journey a lot easier than others.

Hunters can send their pets into battle to tank for them, while Paladins have healing spells to get themselves out of trouble. Other classes, like Rogues, can sneak past enemies and avoid unnecessary fights. It’s also worth considering when each class unlocks its more useful abilities, as some take longer than others to reach their full potential.

Here are my five recommended classes for solo players in World of Warcraft: Forever, including the best leveling specializations and key Forever-specific changes.

One of the biggest advantages of playing a Rogue is that you don't have to fight everything in your path. With Stealth, you can sneak past enemies to reach quest objectives, saving yourself time and potentially a few frustrating deaths.

Taking down a single enemy is rarely the problem for a Rogue. However, you’ll be in trouble when another enemy wanders into the fight, especially if you’ve already used your defensive abilities. I recommend picking off enemies individually and using Stealth to avoid crowded areas whenever possible. There’s no reason to fight half the zone just to collect a quest item.

Combat is recommended for solo leveling, as it provides consistent single-target damage and talents that improve your weapon attacks and survivability.

Your general strategy is to open fights from Stealth, generate Combo Points with abilities like Sinister Strike, and spend them on finishers such as Eviscerate. You can also use Gouge to control an enemy when you need breathing room.

Rogues also received some welcome changes in World of Warcraft: Forever. For starters, Sap no longer breaks Stealth, so you can temporarily disable an enemy without revealing yourself. Poisons have more charges, too, and you can use them alongside temporary weapon enhancements.

You'll also pick up a couple of useful abilities as you level. At level 18, Posed reduces the Energy cost of your next ability when you reach full Energy outside combat. At level 24, Shank gives you another attack that generates Energy, helping you keep attacking instead of waiting for your Energy to recover.

However, Rogues struggle when fighting multiple enemies at once. They lack reliable self-healing and depend on defensive cooldowns, crowd control, and careful positioning to survive.

Combat is a great option if you enjoy picking your battles, but it takes more patience than classes that can comfortably fight multiple enemies at once.

World of Warcraft: Forever is finally live, and while it can be a bit intimidating to start, we've got a few tips to help you out!

One of the best reasons to play a Druid is that you can change how you fight without switching classes. If you’re facing a tough enemy, Bear Form gives you extra protection. Need to get somewhere without fighting everything along the way? Cat Form lets you sneak past enemies instead.

Feral Combat is the recommended solo spec, which focuses on melee damage, mobility, and survivability through Cat Form and Bear Form.

However, Druids can feel relatively slow during the early levels. Bear Form becomes available at level 10, but Cat Form doesn't unlock until level 20. This is where leveling with Feral becomes considerably more enjoyable.

At level 20, you’ll use Energy to attack instead of mana when you unlock Cat Form. This leaves your mana available for healing between fights. As you gain more abilities, you can apply a bleed effect with Rake, use Claw to generate Combo Points, and finish enemies off with your stronger attacks.

Feral Swiftness improves your movement in Cat Form. You can also use Prowl to sneak past unnecessary enemies, similar to a Rogue.

World of Warcraft: Forever also introduces several shapeshifting improvements. Your weapon's damage now contributes directly to your attacks in animal forms, while weapon enhancements and many consumables work without requiring you to transform back.

Additionally, the new Shifting Power talent lets you convert mana into Energy, helping you maintain offensive momentum later in the leveling process.

If an encounter becomes overwhelming, you can switch to Bear Form for additional durability or leave your animal form to heal.

Feral Combat is recommended if you want a class that can handle a little bit of everything. You’ll have plenty of options for getting out of trouble, although learning when to switch forms takes some getting used to.

Unlearn professions in WoW: Forever safely—what resets, how to relearn, plus a UI scale fix if the button's missing.

Paladins are the most forgiving classes for solo players because they combine heavy armor, healing spells, and powerful defensive abilities. You have several ways to recover without relying on another player should you find yourself in a bad situation.

Retribution is the recommended specialization for solo players. You'll focus on melee damage while maintaining the class's defensive strengths.

World of Warcraft: Forever introduces Holy Strike at level six, giving you an instant weapon attack that deals Holy damage. This helps improve early leveling, when Paladin combat can otherwise feel relatively slow.

The Seal and Judgement system has also received an important improvement in Forever. Judgement no longer consumes your active Seal, so you can maintain its effects without repeatedly spending mana to reactivate it.

Your general strategy is to maintain an offensive Seal, use Holy Strike and Judgment to damage enemies, and heal yourself when necessary.

Later in the Retribution talent tree, Twist of Light provides another offensive option. This talent lets your previous Seal temporarily carry over when you activate a different one so that you can benefit from two damage-dealing Seals on your next melee attack.

Protection is another option, particularly if you enjoy fighting multiple enemies. Consecration is now available to every Paladin at level 20, providing area damage that is especially effective against the first four targets.

Retribution is best for questing because most quests have you fighting enemies one at a time, so killing them faster is usually more efficient. You’ll still have access to healing spells and defensive abilities that can save you should you get into a sketchy situation.

Pirate Software’s OnlyFangs raid controversy just got put on blast in a new WoW: Forever trailer.

Warlocks are great for solo players because they can summon demons to fight alongside them while using powerful spells to defeat enemies from a distance.

Affliction is recommended because it is the most efficient leveling specialization, thanks to its combination of damage-over-time effects, self-healing, and resource management.

At level 10, Warlocks can obtain the Voidwalker, giving you a tank that draws enemy attention. This allows you to apply Corruption and Bane of Agony while your demon tanks incoming damage.

World of Warcraft: Forever introduces several improvements that make Affliction more effective. Damage-over-time effects can now crit, while talents like Malediction increase their damage.

The game also separates damaging Banes from utility Curses, allowing you to maintain a Bane of Agony alongside a Curse that weakens your enemies.

However, Affliction's biggest advantage for solo leveling is its ability to sustain itself through consecutive encounters. You can use Drain Life to restore your health, and Life Tap when you need an emergency mana refill.

Affliction also performs well against multiple enemies because you can spread damage-over-time effects across several targets while repositioning.

At level 40, Wrack provides another powerful offensive option. This new Affliction talent channels Shadow damage while making your active Corruption, Siphon Life, and Bane of Agony effects deal periodic damage 100 percent faster.

Affliction is the best choice for general questing because its damage-over-time spells and draining abilities allow you to move between enemies with minimal recovery time. It also lets you fight multiple enemies at once when needed.

Hide your secondary name in World of Warcraft: Forever — quick nameplate steps to keep only your main name visible.

Hunters are the top recommendation for solo players in World of Warcraft: Forever, thanks to their combination of ranged damage, mobility, and animal companions.

Beast Mastery is the recommended specialization. This spec allows you to focus on improving your pets' strength, durability, and damage.

The real fun starts at level 10, once you complete the pet-taming quest chain. Instead of fighting enemies at melee range, your pets will tank for you. You can now attack from a safe range.

Let your pet generate threat before you start shooting. Otherwise, you might find yourself dealing with an angry enemy running straight toward you. Serpent Sting works well against tougher enemies that will survive long enough to take damage over time, while Arcane Shot is useful when you need some immediate damage. If you’re fighting several enemies, make sure your pet isn’t taking more damage than it can handle.

Your pets now gain additional stats from your equipment, meaning your companion gets stronger as you upgrade your gear. This helps keep your pet useful throughout the leveling process.

Beast Mastery also receives new and redesigned talents. Focused Fire improves damage dealt by you and your pet, while Endurance Training increases your pet's durability.

A new ability, Summon Hawk, becomes available after you spend 16 talent points in Beast Mastery. This ability summons a hawk that temporarily attacks your target.

Hunters get improvements to their baseline abilities. Aimed Shot is now available to every Hunter at level 20, while traps can be placed during combat, giving you additional ways to control enemies.

Later, Bestial Wrath provides a powerful offensive cooldown that increases your pet's effectiveness against difficult opponents.

However, Hunters still need to keep their distance, as ranged attacks become unavailable when targets are too close. You must also manage your pet's health, positioning, and abilities.

The Beast Mastery spec offers one of the smoothest solo-leveling experiences available. Your pet can handle much of the danger while you deal consistent damage, making Hunter an excellent choice whether you're new to WoW or jumping from Retail to Forever.

Hunter or Warlock are the best choices if you’re planning to spend most of your time playing solo. Your pet will tank for you, which makes questing a lot less frustrating. Paladin is the best pick if you prefer melee classes instead. With heavy armor and healing spells, you can survive when you pull more enemies than you intended.

It’s also worth giving your chosen class some time before deciding whether to stick with it. Druids, for example, don’t unlock Cat Form until level 20, while Hunters have to reach level 10 before taming their first pet. A class that may feel slow at the beginning might become one of your favorites once you’ve unlocked more of its abilities.

Unlock The New Plague in World of Warcraft: finish the A Recipe for Death chain to access the Undercity quest.

Ocarina of Time Fans Should Check Out This Zelda-Inspired TTRPG That Just Hit Stores

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

After a successful BackerKit campaign, Twilight Sword is now available on retail

The Legend of Zelda fans are eating well right now. The franchise is celebrating its 40th anniversary with the remake of Ocarina of Time, which was released on Nov. 5 to rave reviews and became the second best-selling game on the Nintendo Switch 2 in less than a week. Sony Pictures is releasing a live-action film adaptation in April 2027. Now you can share the feel of exploring Hyrule with friends with the tabletop role-playing game Twilight Sword, which launched in gaming stores this week and has already smashed through the goal for its next crowdfunding campaign.

Italian studio Two Little Mice raised €701,000 ($77,700) for Twilight Sword on BackerKit in 2026, crushing its €10,000 ($12,000) goal in just 24 hours. The game’s co-creators told Polygon they wanted to capture the feeling of Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom at the gaming table, evoking Link’s journey with a modular map revealed by flipping cards representing new regions. The game also has a rich cooking and brewing system, and fast and intuitive combat.

Players take on the role of Champions who have been awakened by the Spirits to protect the land of Radia from the dark knight Vardas and his army of monsters. An original d12-based Created by Twilight rules system revolves around gathering Hope by exploring, helping NPCs, and defeating bosses. Hope is used to counter Despair, the corruption that blocks off areas of the map until players are strong enough to advance.

The $51 Twilight Sword Core Rulebook includes everything you need to create characters, including feats, classes, and equipment. It also provides an overview of the world and some monsters and bosses you might encounter. The $45 Lands of Radia book adds extra character options and everything you need to run a campaign, including travel event tables, landmarks, quest hooks, and key NPCs. The $42 Card Chest includes map pieces to reveal during exploration and cards representing monsters, weapons, feats, and spells to make it easy to keep track of everything. All purchases made through Free League Publishing include a PDF via DrivethruRPG.

Two Little Mice seized on the momentum by launching another BackerKit campaign on the same day as Twilight Sword’s retail release. The Twilight Sword & Monster Book has already raised €371,000 ($411250). Expected to release in October 2027, the book will add more character options and monsters, plus tools for the game master to create their own enemies. It also expands on the crafting system by providing rules to collect monster parts to turn into gear. Add-ons include a GM screen, a Battle Chest with standees representing monsters from all the Twilight Sword books, and a Monster Chest filled with new monster and monster gear cards. The campaign runs through Dec. 3.

BeatBread Brings on Advisors From Meta, Suno

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Industry leaders with expertise in indie music, publishing, streaming, technology, AI and music rights investment will advise on beatBread's future.

By Elizabeth Dilts Marshall

BeatBread is bringing on advisers from Meta, Suno and other music industry experts as its new CEO, Andreea Gleeson, shapes the indie music financing platform’s future, the company said on Thursday (Nov. 12).

The advisory board will include Scott Sellwood, Meta’s director of music business development; Jeremy Sirota, Suno’s chief commercial officer; Johan Ahlström, CEO/co-founder of Kobalt Capital, originally the investment arm of Kobalt; Oana Ruxandra, formerly the investments and partnerships lead for Warner Music Group and now CEO/founder of PopHatch; and Spotify’s former global head of music publishing, Adam Parness.

BeatBread has had a challenging year since the death of its founder and CEO, Peter Sinclair, in 2025. Former TuneCore CEO Gleeson says the advisory group will bring its expertise to help guide beatBread’s thinking on new, more flexible advance and loan products, long-term strategy and market positioning, as the company faces increased competition for providing advances.

“Independent artists, songwriters and music companies are building increasingly sophisticated businesses, but the financial tools available to them haven’t always kept pace,” said Gleeson, who was installed as beatBread’s CEO in September. “I’m excited to have [the advisory board’s] experience and perspectives to help us think through what’s next for beatBread.”

Founded in 2020 to issue capital to independent artists, labels and songwriters who want to maintain ownership of their music, beatBread says it has issued around $100 million in advances through some 2,000 transactions with creators. The company raised $124 million last year in credit and equity capital from Citi, longtime investor Deciens Capital and others to scale the company’s AI-enhanced underwriting technology, chordCashAI, internationally.

In March, beatBread brought on Jameson Toole as board adviser on AI and machine learning. Toole previously was a staff machine‑learning engineer at Spotify and co‑founder of Fritz AI.

Notion users put access keys in AI chats. A supplier breach exposed them

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Exposed AI chats contained access keys, prompting Notion to disable them and warn affected customers to replace other pasted credentials.

Notion disabled exposed API keys on Nov. 11 after finding them in AI chats affected by a breach at supplier Fireworks AI, according to its updated security advisory. These keys let software access workspace data within the permissions their owners granted.

Notion says the incident affected fewer than 1% of its workspaces. The exposed dataset included Notion AI chat transcripts used for its Insights feature.

For the vast majority of affected workspaces, those transcripts lacked labels identifying who wrote the chats or which workspace they belonged to, and contained no tool-call results. Information inside the messages could still connect a conversation to a user, workspace or company, however. The API keys found in affected messages show why missing ownership labels still leave secrets in the text.

Notion found API keys inside affected Insights messages and revoked exposed Notion keys on Nov. 11.

Revocation addressed exposed keys inside messages.

Schematic illustration. No real credential is shown, and key misuse is not established.

Schematic transcript cross-section connects absent ownership labels to a credential retained in message text, then to the reported November 11 revocation. No real transcript or credential is reproduced.

Schematic illustration. No real credential is shown, and key misuse is not established.

Notion's API security guidance says someone holding a token can make requests within its capabilities and content permissions, potentially exposing or changing workspace content. An internal connection's access is limited to pages shared with it and its assigned capabilities.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The key revocations came after Notion's initial disconnection from Fireworks. After a Nov. 2 notification appeared to involve synthetic testing data only, Notion says it disconnected its systems, deleted data held in the Fireworks system and stopped communications by the morning of Nov. 3.

Fireworks then notified Notion of potential customer-data access on Nov. 6 and confirmed the new dataset on Nov. 8. Notion identified the Insights transcripts in that material.

Notion has disabled Insights during its investigation and stopped serving open-weight models through Fireworks. It says no other Notion features were affected. Its advisory does not report misuse of the Notion keys covered by the Nov. 11 revocation.

In its Nov. 9 incident update, Fireworks described impacts to resource metadata and billing information, as well as secrets, datasets and incomplete model weights for a limited set of accounts.

Fireworks separately says it has detected no impact to Inference, Training SDK or Nexus, services that have zero data retention by default. Those findings remain provisional while its investigation continues.

For affected Notion customers, the company's advice extends beyond the keys it revoked. It recommends reviewing the impacted data securely inside the workspace, treating that material as exposed and rotating passwords, API keys or other credentials pasted into Notion AI during the incident timeframe. Notion says it has contacted customers it identified as affected.

This Simple Storage Trick Can Keep Lettuce Fresh for a Month

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The trick to keeping lettuce fresh for a month is already in your kitchen.

There are few things more irritating than throwing away food you had every intention of eating. Fresh produce is especially frustrating because it seems to operate on its own schedule. You buy a beautiful head of lettuce, imagining all the fresh salads and sandwiches you’ll make that week, only to find a head of wilted leaves waiting for you when you finally get around to it.

Enter Chef Charles Kim, better known as Chef Fatty on social media. He has an impressive knack for making me rethink my cooking. He’s already changed how I caramelize onions and make the juiciest chicken breasts, and now he’s apparently coming for my crisper drawer. His trick promises to keep lettuce fresh for an entire month using nothing more than aluminum foil—a claim I couldn’t resist investigating.

The idea is simple: Instead of leaving your head of lettuce in the plastic produce bag, loosely wrap it in aluminum foil before putting it in the refrigerator. That’s it. No special containers, paper towels or elaborate produce-preserving rituals required.

Chef Fatty recommends removing any yellowing or deteriorating leaves before wrapping the lettuce. If it’s dirty, you can wash it first, but make sure it’s completely dry. Then loosely fold the foil around the head and place it in the refrigerator’s crisper drawer.

The reasoning has to do with moisture and airflow. Lettuce loses its crispness as water escapes from its leaves, but trapping too much moisture can also encourage decay. A loose foil wrapping strikes just the right balance, protecting the leaves from drying out without sealing them inside an airtight container.

I wanted to see the difference for myself, so I bought two heads of romaine lettuce at the same time. I left one in the plastic produce bag from the grocery store and loosely wrapped the other in aluminum foil. Both went into the same crisper drawer in my refrigerator, where I left them undisturbed for a month. When I finally pulled them out, the difference was unmistakable.

After 30 days in the refrigerator, the foil-wrapped romaine was in dramatically better shape than its plastic-bag counterpart. The lettuce stored in its original bag had deteriorated considerably, while the foil-wrapped head had retained much more of its original appearance and crispness.

I had to peel away several of the outer leaves of the plastic-bag lettuce before I found anything resembling a crisp texture. The foil-wrapped head wasn’t perfect, but it was still pretty crisp, with leaves so fresh that I happily turned them into a Caesar salad for lunch. This was an entire month after I’d bought it. I’d call that a successful experiment.

While I tested the method with romaine, it’s also worth trying on other lettuce varieties. How long each stays fresh will depend on its condition when purchased, the refrigerator it’s stored in, and the variety itself. And lettuce isn’t the only vegetable that might benefit. Taste of Home senior editor Lindsay Mattison told me she uses the same aluminum foil trick to store celery, which makes sense given its tendency to lose moisture and go limp in the refrigerator.

I wouldn’t necessarily recommend buying lettuce with the intention of ignoring it for a month, and it’s still important to check for slime, mold or other signs of spoilage before eating it. But knowing I can give my produce a much longer window before it ends up in the compost is reason enough to keep an extra roll of foil handy.

How To Get In The Same Phase In World Of Warcraft: Forever

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Stop losing friends to phasing in World of Warcraft: Forever — quick world refresh tips to get your party back in Azeroth.

Playing World of Warcraft: Forever with friends is a great way to tackle difficult quests and explore Azeroth. However, joining the same party doesn't always mean you can see each other.

The game uses multiple copies of outdoor zones, commonly called phases or layers, to manage its player population. Because of this, you might find yourself standing in the same location as a party member without being able to see or interact with them. Fortunately, you can move into the same phase without leaving your party.

When you join a party, look near your chat window for a blue notification stating that the world around you will refresh. This message may include a countdown, but you don't have to wait for the timer to expire.

If you previously dismissed the notification, look for a small arrow near your chat window to reopen it.

Unlike some earlier versions of World of Warcraft, joining a party doesn't automatically move you into the same phase as the rest of your party. The refresh system lets you choose when to make that transition.

It's best to refresh somewhere safe because enemies may appear in different locations after changing phases.

Hide your secondary name in World of Warcraft: Forever — quick nameplate steps to keep only your main name visible.

If you're still having trouble joining your friends, here are a few things that have helped me while playing with friends.

First, crossing a zone boundary can trigger a phase change and put your party members into the same version of the world.

When that doesn't work, log out and back in while remaining in the party. This also helped my party members appear on the same phase as everyone else.

Once everyone is visible, you can finally get back to questing together instead of wondering why your friends have vanished into another dimension. If you run into the same problem later, check for the World Refresh notification before leaving your party. There’s usually no reason to disband your group just to get everyone together.

Unlearn professions in WoW: Forever safely—what resets, how to relearn, plus a UI scale fix if the button's missing.

New Research Shows How Much Altitude Actually Slows You Down

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

One of the all-time great topics for discussion on the Sunday long run is “What was so-and-so’s race performance really worth?” Andreas Almgren, for example, won this year’s New York Marathon by running the first half in 1:04:04 and the second half, including the hills of Central Park, in 1:01:57. What could he have run with a flatter course and more even pacing? Similarly, you can ponder the effects of factors like heat, wind, drafting, shoe design… and altitude.

Everyone understands that the thin air at high altitudes matters when you’re climbing Mount Everest, or even playing football in Denver. But the effects can also show up at much lower altitudes. I once spent a few months in Australia’s capital city, Canberra, which is only around 1,900 feet above sea level. I had a streak of disappointing road races—which only made sense once I realized that even at that altitude VO2 max is reduced by about five percent. Australian sports scientists had figured that out when they were setting up the Australian Institute of Sport in Canberra and noticed that their fancy new testing equipment seemed to produce abnormally low values.

This intrinsic worth of high-altitude performances is currently a hot topic because the NCAA voted over the summer to eliminate its altitude conversions for track and field qualifying standards. Until now, any performance at more than 3,000 feet above sea level was adjusted to account for the effects of less air resistance and lower oxygen levels. Sprint performances had time added, because thin air makes them faster; middle- and long-distance performances had time deducted, because the lack of oxygen made them slower.

The conversions were eliminated in part because, as the rules committee wrote, “there is no true science being applied” in formulating them. That makes a new study in the International Journal of Sports Physiology and Performance slightly late to the party—but it’s interesting nonetheless. Atle Guttormsen and Simen Guttormsen of the Norwegian University of Life Sciences analyzed more than 900,000 individual performances from track meets at 382 NCAA indoor track venues over a 14-year period to determine exactly how much difference altitude makes.

The statistical analysis attempted to account for as many sources of variation as possible: the design of the track (e.g. banked or unbanked), how a given performance compared to the athlete’s other performances that season, how early or late in the season it was, and so on. (Conveniently, there’s no wind indoors.) Overall, they came up with an estimate for how much altitude affected various events per 1,000 meters of altitude (which is 3,281 feet).

Here’s the resulting data for six running events:

Up to 400 meters, athletes are faster at altitude. The effect at 60 meters is slightly smaller because (I’m assuming) they spend a large fraction of the race accelerating to top speed. At 200 meters, they’re about 0.4 to 0.5 percent faster than they would be at sea level. In the long jump, triple jump, and pole vault, all of which are speed-based (but not shown above), there’s a similar advantage.

The crossover, where lack of oxygen begins to outweigh lower aerodynamic drag, happens at race distances just beyond 400 meters. The negative effects of altitude climb steeply at distances of up to 3,000 meters, then level off. That’s because in these longer events, aerodynamic drag is mostly irrelevant, so the effects of altitude are purely negative. For distances of 3,000 meters and beyond, the negative effects of altitude in this dataset seem to be about 2.7 percent for every 1,000 meters of altitude. Note that 1,000 meters is just slightly more than 3,000 feet, where the NCAA’s altitude conversions used to kick in. That equates to a roughly 30-second disadvantage for 5,000-meter runners.

The most interesting point in this graph is the 800-meter data. Women experience a greater disadvantage than men at altitude: 0.92 percent versus 0.50 percent. Top NCAA men are running about 1:45, while top women are running about 2:00. Since they’re so close to the point where oxygen and aerodynamics balance out, that 15-second difference matters. The men are going faster, so they benefit more from the reduction in drag; the women are running for longer, so they suffer more from the lack of oxygen. The Guttormsens argue that the NCAA needs sex-specific conversion factors—but the argument is moot since the conversion factors have been scrapped, at least for now.

Assuming the oxygen-related penalty stays roughly constant at 2.7 percent beyond 3,000 meters, here’s what kind of slowdown you can expect in endurance events at various elevations:

The upshot: I can now tell myself that I was about 1.6 percent fitter than my half-marathon times in Canberra suggested. (I’ve been saying that for years anyway… but now I’ve got science!)

The problem is that altitude affects everyone differently. A few years ago, I wrote about a neat study showing that altitude-born South American cyclists perform relatively better in high-altitude race stages than European cyclists. Even though the Europeans were doing lots of altitude training camps, they couldn’t overcome the disadvantage of being born near sea level. Altitude response depends on a mix of genetics (where your ancestors lived), environment (where you live and/or train), and developmental factors (where you were born and grew up). This is another reason the NCAA cited in scrapping altitude conversion factors: the “right” conversion is different for everyone.

There’s even some evidence that trained endurance athletes are more susceptible to the negative effects of altitude than untrained people. That may be because their hearts pump blood so powerfully that it rushes past the lungs too quickly to pick up a full load of oxygen. This affects some athletes even at sea level, but becomes increasingly common as you climb in elevation.

Personally, Canberra isn’t the only history I’d like to revise: some of the most disappointing races of my life took place in Calgary (3,428 feet) and Edmonton (2,116 feet). These aren’t cities that anyone thinks of as “high-altitude,” but it’s clear that their elevation does have an effect—and that effect is bigger if you don’t realize that it exists, because in trying to stick with your expected pace you can burn yourself out early in the race. Knowing about these effects matters, and the new data gives you a starting point to make adjustments next time you head to the mountains—or even just the foothills.

For more Sweat Science, sign up for the email newsletter and check out my new book The Explorer’s Gene: Why We Seek Big Challenges, New Flavors, and the Blank Spots on the Map.

Thailand proposes $148K daily cap on stablecoin deposits and withdrawals

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Thailand’s SEC would set separate daily caps for stablecoin deposits and withdrawals and require customers to use wallets they own.

Thailand’s securities regulator has proposed capping daily stablecoin deposits and withdrawals at 5 million baht ($148,000) each per customer at regulated crypto operators.

The limit would apply separately to deposits and withdrawals at each operator, Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) said in a Thursday announcement. The regulator said the rules aim to curb money laundering, cybercrime and attempts to bypass cross-border money transfer rules.

The draft would bar stablecoin transfers to and from third-party wallets. Operators would have to verify that customers own the accounts or wallets they use for deposits and withdrawals.

Operators would have to comply with the Travel Rule, which requires crypto service providers to collect and share information about transfers, and use blockchain analytics to identify wallets linked to suspicious activity.

The draft would require crypto exchanges to name their market makers and identify the assets for which they provide liquidity. Crypto brokers would be barred from using liquidity providers for stablecoin-baht pairs. Certain off-platform trades would be subject to a 3 million baht minimum.

The draft follows a September consultation in which the SEC proposed stablecoin transfer limits and tighter oversight of crypto operators.

United Airlines Bans AI Booking Agents, But Who Is It Really Protecting?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

United Airlines is restricting third-party AI assistants from booking and managing tickets, citing privacy and safety concerns. I think there is a much more compelling commercial explanation: United understands what happens when passengers have an assistant that continually watches fares and captures savings after they book. The airline is getting ahead of that possibility, and I find its attempt to present this as customer protection rather unconvincing.

In a November 11 announcement, United said it is restricting unauthorized third-party personal AI agents across its digital platforms and through travel agencies and aggregators.

The airline warns that sharing credentials with these tools could expose sensitive information or lead to unwanted purchases and account changes. It also raises regulatory concerns, pointing to the acknowledgment passengers must make concerning hazardous materials during check-in.

Those concerns deserve consideration. But they do not adequately explain why an assistant should be prohibited from making a change that I understand, authorize, and could make myself.

United has added the following to its prohibited ticketing practices:

“The use of AI Agents to make, modify or service a Ticket, and/or to purchase any service or product.”

The definition encompasses autonomous and semi-autonomous assistants, including those whose actions a passenger supervises or approves.

We already have tools that monitor airfare and notify us when prices change. I use fare alerts myself. But receiving an alert still leaves me with work to do. I must examine the offer, check the restrictions, and determine whether changing the ticket actually leaves me better off.

Imagine an assistant doing that work continuously, then completing an authorized change whenever the savings justify it based on the parameters I input at the start of the process…something I would happily use.

Consider a hypothetical $800 ticket whose rules allow you to rebook at a lower fare and retain the difference as a future flight credit, with no change fee.

The price drops to $650. Your AI assistant identifies the same flight and fare conditions, completes the permitted change, and secures a $150 credit.

Later, the price drops to $575. It does the calculation again and captures another $75.

You still take the flight you intended to take, but now have $225 available toward future travel.

That is not a cash refund on a nonrefundable ticket, and the actual opportunity depends on the fare rules. But evaluating those complications is precisely what would make the technology useful. Tell it what you want to preserve and when it may act. Let it calculate whether the lower price represents actual savings.

Airline revenue management benefits from passengers behaving differently. Some book early, some wait, and some keep checking after buying. Many understandably consider the transaction finished once the confirmation email arrives.

An assistant that keeps shopping changes that calculation.

A brief fare reduction that I would otherwise miss becomes an opportunity and a small saving that would not justify 20 minutes of my time may become worthwhile when software handles the work. Multiply that across millions of reservations and you can understand why an airline might become uncomfortable.

It would put serious pressure on the ability to retain a higher fare from someone whose ticket permits a lower-price exchange. The airline could still set its prices and restrictions, but fewer customers would leave available savings unclaimed merely because they were busy living their lives.

I suspect United understands this very well. This strikes me as a preemptive move to control who can manage a reservation before automated consumer tools become an ordinary part of travel.

These new rules do not suggest that United intends to eliminate every fare alert: the more consequential restriction is preventing the assistant from acting on what it finds.

I would not hand any application unrestricted control of my MileagePlus account and yes, I do not want software canceling an important trip because it misunderstood an instruction. But those are reasons to establish permissions and accountability.

United’s hazardous-materials example is particularly weak as a justification for prohibiting reservation management. The federal passenger-notification rule requires disclosures and passenger acknowledgment in specified circumstances. Reading that requirement, I do not see why an assistant could not stop and ask the traveler to complete the necessary acknowledgment personally.

More to the point, checking whether my ticket can be exchanged for a lower fare does not require an assistant to make a declaration about what is inside my suitcase. A responsible system should require approval where needed and limit what software can do. United’s restriction still encompasses an assistant whose actions I supervise and approve.

So no, I do not accept the customer-protection explanation.

Scott Kirby offered a more revealing observation at a Chicago event, according to The Wall Street Journal:

“To be honest, I don’t want to create another middleman.”

I understand why United would prefer to control the customer relationship, but my interests are not identical to United’s. I may want someone working for me, comparing the options and continuing to look after my reservation once United has collected payment.

I recently examined Kirby’s argument that technology helps make United the best airline in the world. I agree that its app is excellent. Better technology has made my United travel easier. But I also want technology that helps me spend less. An assistant that understands my preferences and captures an eligible fare reduction would be doing exactly that.

United should explain what secure, authorized access would require. Simply blocking an AI assistant carte blanche protects the airline’s control much more clearly than it protects my interests.

United says its restrictions on third-party AI agents protect customers. I think they also–and primarily–protect United from a future in which passengers can monitor and manage reservations far more effectively than they do today.

Just as airlines use complex algorithms to extract the highest fare fro Mme, I want to use technology to help capture savings without having to babysit every reservation.

To me, this looks like a cheap shot dressed up as customer protection. United is entitled to secure its systems, but I am far less sympathetic to using that explanation to block an authorized assistant from helping me obtain a better deal.

Join our mailing list for a daily summary of posts! We never sell your info.

Matthew is an avid traveler who calls Los Angeles home. Each year he travels more than 200,000 miles by air and has visited more than 135 countries. Working both in the aviation industry and as a travel consultant, Matthew has been featured in major media outlets around the world and uses his Live and Let's Fly blog to share the latest news in the airline industry, commentary on frequent flyer programs, and detailed reports of his worldwide travel.

The correct perspective, Mattthew. Well articulated.

I use an Ai agent to keep tabs on hotel reservations as they often go down over time. I have saved literally thousands each year doing this. While I tend more towards the self gardening of my flights it is clear that United wants to prevent consumers from fighting back against the airline’s algorithms. Its dirty. Especially given the rise in fares and the destruction of loyalty.

“Someone” can be held accountable when something goes wrong. Technology that tracks and alerts you of fare changes has existed for a while. AI agents are a whole other can of worms.

Baldini names Italy U21 starting XI against France 24 hours early

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The Italy U21 side will take on France in a friendly on Friday evening, and Azzurrini head coach Silvio Baldini has already selected his starting XI, led by Milan teenager Francesco Camarda at centre-forward.

The Italy U21 squad will be in action against France and Belgium in a pair of international friendlies over the next few days, following their successful qualification for the U21 European Championships during the previous international break.

Baldini has a squad of 25 players available to select from. Those players are as follows:

Goalkeepers: Giovanni Daffara (Parma), Diego Mascardi (Torino), Lorenzo Palmisani (Frosinone), Massimo Pessina (Bologna);

Defenders: Davide Bartesaghi (Milan), Fabio Chiarodia (Borussia Mönchengladbach), Pietro Comuzzo (Torino), Eddy Kouadio (Monza), Emanuele Lulli (Roma), Filippo Mane (Borussia Dortmund), Brando Moruzzi (Avellino);

Italy U21 starting XI vs. France: Palmisani; Mane, Comuzzo, Chiarodia, Bartesaghi; Lipani, Amatucci, Dagasso; Fini, Camarda, Cacciamani.

“They’vs all trained well,” said Baldini during his pre-match press conference on Thursday.

“We’ve got two matches with not a lot of time to recover. I will evaluate whether to make substitutions during the match, or leave a block of players for the game in Laval and another block for the game against Belgium.”

He continued: “We’re a family here. I compare it to when somebody works abroad, but returns home to celebrations and a warm welcome. I feel those same emotions when I see these guys. They know how to separate the hard work we do on the pitch and the fun we have off it. There are rules, but the first one is to be joyful. I want to see them laugh and joke. For me, watching them is a gift.”

Looking ahead to the test against France, Baldini added: “France are a strong team, but we don’t care who we face. We care about being free and playing the way we want to play. We have to do our thing on the ball. If they don’t allow us to, we also know how to make sacrifices, to recover and keep the spaces tight.”
