Women finally start to get pride of place with central London sculptures

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Tschabalala Self’s Lady in Blue (2026) is the 16th Fourth Plinth commission Photo: Jeff Spicer/PA Media Assignments

London bristles with sculptures of men. Only 4% of public monuments in the city are devoted to named women, according to the charity Art UK. It says something about the national psyche that there are twice as many animal statues in the city as there are sculptures devoted to specific women. But right now a pair of radical new works depicting and made by women—Tschabalala Self’s Lady in Blue in Trafalgar Square, and Nicole Eisenman’s Stack outside the Royal Academy of Arts (RA) on Piccadilly—are making their presence powerfully felt in two of the most male-dominated locations in the capital.

In Trafalgar Square, surrounded by depictions of monarchs and militia and across from Admiral Nelson atop his 51m-high column, Self’s Lady in Blue painted bronze cuts a distinctive dash on the empty plinth to the northwest of the square that, since 1999, has hosted a programme of contemporary sculpture commissions. Head held aloft with her vivid blue dress clinging to her ample curves, she strides forth in high heels, exuding purposeful confidence and optimism. As with all Fourth Plinth sculptures, this impressive female will be in situ for two years, before going to a permanent home in Fornebu in Norway.

“She’s not an idol to venerate or a historic figure to commemorate, she’s an everyday young metropolitan woman much like those who use the square every day,” Self declared at the work’s unveiling, adding: “Lady in Blue is not static, she’s in motion… and I hope she inspires all who see her to find the optimism to move forward in community with all those around them.” In this spirit, Self sees her sculpture as an attempt to redress both racial and gender imbalance in public art whether in her native US, the UK or Norway. “In most cities there’s an underrepresentation of sculptures that depict women, and especially women of colour, so I think that it is important to add more images and visual representation,” she said.

Meanwhile, a few streets away outside the RA, which in 258 years has shown only two living women in its main galleries, New York-based Eisenman has erected Stack, a towering work at over 8m tall. This totem of figures and objects made from an assortment of materials, including bronze, oak, brass, steel, nylon, acrylic resin and tin, is currently having a friendly face-off with Alfred Drury’s grandiose bronze sculpture of Joshua Reynolds, the RA’s first president, who brandishes his paintbrush and easel just outside the main entrance. In 2021 Eisenman got to the shortlist for Trafalgar Square’s Fourth Plinth and now with Stack, she has created her own column, impressive in its scale and magnitude, but also irreverently anti-monumental.

Nicole Eisenman's Stack (2022-24), installed in the courtyard of the Royal Academy

Photo: Damien Griffiths. © Nicole Eisenman. Courtesy the artist and Hauser & Wirth

At its base hunches a craggy, androgynous figure shod in Birkenstocks and with a wooden barrel strapped to its back. A stand-in for the artist, perhaps, abject and emphatically earthbound, clutching not a paintbrush but an empty wine glass. On top of the barrel, rising out of a puddle of what looks like gooey melted cheese is a giant potato, over which lolls a rusty leonine cat modelled on Edie, Eisenman’s studio companion. Rather than a posturing war hero, Stack is topped off by the amiably smiling seated figure of the late artist Phyllida Barlow, rendered in gleaming sky-blue aluminium and jauntily balancing a vase of flowers on her head.

Barlow, who died unexpectedly in 2023, was a Royal Academician. Given where this work is situated, it is hard not to see Stack as a memorial to this much loved and greatly mourned artist. Eisenman is a huge admirer of Barlow, whose colossally ambitious but always defiantly anti-hubristic sculpture she first encountered when they were both in the Carnegie International in 2013. They subsequently became friends to the extent that an apparently amused Barlow went to Eisenman’s New York studio in 2022 to be 3D-printed for this work—“She was really good humoured about it,” Eisenman recalled in a recent talk at the Royal Institution. But while undoubtedly honouring Barlow, Stack is also more than a tribute to one person.

Eisenman is reluctant to offer specific interpretations but in her talk she revealed that the work was conceived with London in mind, declaring: “It’s amazing to me that it is at the RA. I hope it can stay in London.” She also stated that Stack emerged during the pandemic and forms a personal testament to when, as she put it, “life got pared down to the basics: food, wine, art, my cat”.

In strikingly different ways but with similar underlying intent, Stack and Lady in Blue offer alternative monuments to aspects of all our lives that rarely get acknowledged, and most notably the power of great women of all ages and from all origins. May we see more of their kind in all our cities.

Questions raised about the fate of the capital’s most prominent platform for contemporary art

New version of sculpture that was first shown at Skulptur Projekte Münster in 2017 is the artist’s first permanent public work

U.S. Pilot Arrested At London Heathrow Airport, Admits Cocaine Possession—Passengers Owed Over $150,000 Compensation

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

A 64-year-old U.S. pilot arrested at London Heathrow has pleaded guilty to possessing cocaine. Wayne Sigler, from Louisiana, was remanded in custody and faces two additional charges involving alleged drunkenness or unfitness for duty. According to the BBC’s report of information confirmed by the Metropolitan Police, Sigler was arrested on Sunday, November 1. He appeared in court on Saturday, November 7, and pleaded guilty to possessing cocaine. His next hearing is scheduled for December 7 at Isleworth Crown Court. Those charges fall under the Railways and Transport Safety Act 2003. The law also covers preparing to fly, duties after reporting for work, and standby. The charges don’t establish that he flew passengers while impaired. Reporting on the incident doesn’t identify the airline or flight, but there’s a Wayne Sigler who identifies as an American Airlines pilot in federal campaign donation records, and online discussion pegs him as an American pilot who came to the airline via America West and US Airways. On November 1, American Airlines 39 to Miami and American AIrlines 91 to Chicago O’Hare were both cancelled, and American 85 to O’Hare departed nearly 5 hours late. What Delayed Passengers Are Owed A flight departing Heathrow is covered by U.K. passenger compensation rules regardless of where the airline is based. For a journey over 3,500 kilometers, such as London to the U.S., a qualifying arrival delay of more than four hours means £520 per person (~ US$690.42). A three-to-four-hour delay falls into the £260 band. An airline can avoid the compensation payment when extraordinary circumstances caused the disruption. But the U.K. Supreme Court’s 2024 decision in Lipton v BA Cityflyer rejected that defense in the case of a pilot’s illness. And paragraph 170 specifically addresses a pilot who drinks enough to be unfit to report for work. If that causes a cancellation, the court says the reason is “inherent in the airline’s activity and operations.” A plane with 250 passengers at £520 each would mean £130,000 in compensation or $172,500. There was a British Airways pilot who bragged about using cocaine during a Johannesburg layover. A flight attendant reported his messages, he was kept from operating the return flight, and the airline fired him. One Thing Nathan Fielder Found Interesting About Elizabeth Holmes That Wasn't In You Can See Everything

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

A Lot Of Elizabeth Holmes' Former Co-Workers Have Now Seen You Can See Everything: 'He Fell For It Too'

You Can See Everything Has So Many Close-Ups Of Elizabeth Holmes’ Face. Nathan Fielder Explains Why

I've Been Stumped Over Elizabeth Holmes Turning Down Money For You Can See Everything, But There May Be An Unavoidable Reason

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It’s not just the deceit. It's how deep it runs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s documentary from A24 about Elizabeth Holmes, You Can See Everything, is all about the truth. The central question that we, as the audience, are asking the entire time, who is telling the truth, who is lying, and just what is the truth. Even though Fielder spent weeks living with Holmes and her partner just prior to the former Theranos founder reporting to prison in 2023, even he still doesn’t have a grasp of how much Holmes is deceiving people, or what she actually believes. He talked about it in a recent interview, and it both makes things clearer and more opaque at the same time, just like the movie.

In the documentary, Fielder dances around how he feels about Holmes’ statements, like being incredulous when she claims that she’s never lied about anything at all in her life. By design, he rarely calls Holmes on her lies (with a couple of exceptions). He also doesn’t get into the definition of the truth. He leaves that up to us as the audience. Specifically, he doesn’t address whether Holmes even knows if she is lying or not. He addressed this with Wired recently, saying:

It almost feels, at times, that it’s not so black-and-white what’s going on in her head. A lie, a classic lie, is someone who’s like, ‘OK, I am saying something that I’m consciously knowing is not true in this moment.’ But it’s not just the words, it’s the design of every aspect of her life that feels like a part of what is going on.

He also doesn’t quite have his head around just how much of her whole life is wrapped up in the deceit? He adds:

What’s interesting about her to me, I think, just goes beyond that in any given moment … I can’t piece it together. She makes these claims that are like—how could anyone believe this or say they’ve never done this? Is it a lie in that moment? Is it a lie if she believes it to be true?

This is something that has long been discussed and pontificated upon when it comes to the truth and people who spend their loves deceiving people. Do dishonest people like Holmes either know they are lying, or are they so calculating that almost everything becomes a lie, or at minimum a tall tale? Fielder doesn’t seem to have any answers, even after spending all that time with Holmes, as she told him those stories.

One of the most impressive things about You Can See Everything, which is still tearing up the box office on the 2026 movie schedule. is that the movie lives on in the viewers' brains for days or weeks after seeing it. I mean, I saw this movie a month ago, and I still think about it and want to talk about it all the time. Like lies, when you have to peel layers off of them like an onion to get to the truth, this film is much the same way. To mix metaphors, every thread you tug on pulls out more layers to peel back.

Noctua brings space-grade carbon nanotube thermal pad to AMD AM5 and AM4 processors

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Austrian cooling outfit Noctua is officially rolling its new NT-CP1 AM5/4 Carbice carbon nanotube thermal pad for AMD AM4 and AM5 processors into the retail market. After announcing its partnership with advanced nanotechnology company Carbice back in June 2026, the premium thermal pad can now be purchased for $24.95 in the US and €24.95 in the European region.

Conventional thermal pastes and most thermal pads gradually lose performance over time due to pump-out, delamination, cracking, and other forms of degradation. However, Noctua says that its new NT-CP1 thermal pad takes a different approach. It makes use of Carbice's vertically-aligned carbon nanotube technology, originally developed for high thermal reliability in aerospace systems. The carbon nanotubes gradually conform to microscopic imperfections of a CPU’s IHS over hundreds to thousands of thermal cycles.

This allows heat transfer performance to improve during the initial activation period rather than gradually deteriorating. The thermal pad can also maintain stable, maintenance-free performance for years once the initial activation phase is complete. Thus, making it a suitable option for users who want consistent thermal performance without having to periodically replace degraded thermal paste.

"Carbice pads offer a unique combination of ultimate performance stability and convenient installation. This makes the NT-CP1 the perfect choice for everyone who’s looking for a thermal pad that’s super easy to apply and never needs to be replaced," said Noctua CEO Roland Mossig in a press note.

Unlike conventional carbon or graphite-based thermal pads, which can be brittle, slippery, and electrically conductive, the NT-CP1 features an aluminum backbone sandwiched between layers of carbon nanotubes. The surface is also protected by a nanoscale polymer coating, which Noctua says makes the pad more robust, easier to handle, and less likely to move during installation. The company recommends a simple peel, press, and peel installation process, while the slightly tacky coating helps keep the pad in place and offers electrical insulation as long as the polymer layer remains intact.

With an ultra-thin 90µm profile and precut 33x33mm size, the thermal pad fits both AM5 and AM4 socket processors. Noctua also highlights its manufacturing process, noting that the pad is produced in the US using recycled aluminum and waste carbon gas to grow the carbon nanotubes. The company claims that its manufacturing process is more energy-efficient compared to conventional graphite and graphene-based pads, while the aluminum structure allows the product to be recycled through standard aluminum recycling channels.

WoW: Forever's 'Pointless' Early Quest Reward Hides a Much Bigger Secret

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The Ancient Heirloom ring, awarded to every Skyborne character, is part of a massive quest chain players are still solving

If you start a Skyborne character in World of Warcraft: Forever — one of the blue sky elves unlocked by purchasing a DLC pack — you're awarded an item called the Ancient Heirloom very early on in the game. This ring can be equipped, but it has no stats and no apparent purpose. It's odd to receive a ring at a low level, because these items aren't usually found until later in the game; when I received one during the beta test, I figured it was just there to add a bit of flavor to the new race and didn't give it a second thought.

How wrong I was. It turns out that the ring is an instrumental part of WoW: Forever's first big secret: a huge, game-spanning questline that players have yet to see to its conclusion.

Known as the Dawn Waker Secret, the sequence was discovered by the WoW Secret Finding community on Discord, and there's a step-by-step rundown available on Wowhead. It begins at level 1 on Zephras Isle, the starting zone of the Skyborne, and extends beyond level 40 (so far). If you don't have access to the Skyborne, it's still possible to travel to Zephras Isle and find the ring lying around in a building.

The questline involves finding a special torch on the body of a guard in Duskwood, using this to uncover an ancient sword hilt on Zephras Isle, forging the hilt into a fancy sword, purifying a water globe, and much more, as you tour various spots in the game with significance to Warcraft's most ancient elven lore, including the new Mount Hyjal high-level zone. The Ancient Heirloom ring is important, because it must be equipped for the player to see and interact with elven spirits crucial to the quest, or to reveal ancient elven vaults.

Blizzard's fresh take on classic WoW is going great, but the Skyborne show just how hard it is to add new stuff to an old game

So far, the trail of breadcrumbs ends with a character called Prince Tortheldrin (an ancient, cruel elven prince), who's to be found hanging out in the Dire Maul dungeon. The Prince's dialogue suggests players will need to reach Honored reputation with the Shen'dralar faction in order to hear more.

The sequence seems to revolve around two legendary blades, Eluvi'ana (the sword discovered on Zephras Isle) and Eluvi'tor. Perhaps one of these — or both, in upgraded form — will be the chain's ultimate reward. Whatever happens, it's a great way to tie the Skyborne into the history of Warcraft's Highborne elves, and to pay off a pointless-seeming quest reward from WoW: Forever's earliest moments.

4 Man Utd Signings Michael Carrick Recommended This Summer

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The news is now official: Michael Carrick has been sacked as Manchester United manager. USMNT manager Mauricio Pochettino is set to replace the Englishman, with GIVEMESPORT sources previously revealing that an agreement in principle over personal terms has been reached.

It will come as no surprise to football fans that Carrick has lost his job. The now-fired coach endured a terrible start to the season, and departs with Man Utd languishing inside the relegation zone, with one win from their opening 10 Premier League games.

Amid the poor results, friction around the club has been apparent, with senior star Lisandro Martinez questioning the summer recruitment at Old Trafford in an explosive interview. It's now emerged that Carrick recommended four signings in the summer transfer window, but the board failed to land any of them.

Carrick's four wanted men are all of high quality and surely would have helped keep him in the job at Man Utd for longer than the 10 league games he managed. Journalist Henry Winter revealed the four footballers, writing on X:

"Sir Jim Ratcliffe will one day have to explain why Manchester United didn’t follow Michael Carrick’s recommendation of the four players he felt the club needed in the summer window – Elliot Anderson, Lewis Hall, Morgan Rogers and, fourth on the list, Mateus Fernandes. Were debt repayments restricting their buying power? Was it simply because United felt the fees were too much for them?"

Mauricio Pochettino has been told by Paul Scholes that he must forget Manchester United pair who will sabotage him.

Anderson was snapped up by Manchester City, with the Etihad side paying £116m to land the English midfielder. Rogers joined Chelsea for £1m more, setting a new record as the most expensive British signing of all time.

Maybe it would be harsh to suggest Man Utd failed massively in not being willing or able to pay such high sums for those two, but it's a sign of the times that the Red Devils were so easily beaten to the star duo by their Premier League rivals.

And there are fewer excuses for Fernandes. While the Portuguese midfileder left West Ham for Tottenham Hotspur, Man Utd were understood to be in the race. No deal was done, though, as Carrick watched another target move to a different club, this time for £85m – with Spurs showing far more ambition in the summer transfer window.

As for Hall, he didn't even move. Instead, Newcastle United convinced him that his immediate future is better served at St James' Park, with the defender signing a contract in September. The Red Devils have needed a left-back replacement for the ageing Luke Shaw, and Hall looks to be the perfect fit. However, as with the three aforementioned, Carrick was once again let down by the club hierarchy.

Khloe Kardashian Says Lamar Odom Is "Dead” to Her After Documentary

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Khloe Kardashian said during the Nov. 12 episode of The Kardashians that Lamar Odom has burned his final bridge with her after his comments in his Untold documentary.

Some things, you just don’t come back from.

And for Khloe Kardashian, her ex-husband Lamar Odom’s characterization of their relationship in his Untold Netflix documentary was a bridge too far.

The topic is brought up in the Nov. 12 epsiode of The Kardashians by Scott Disick, who asked, “Hey, did you hear what Lamar said about this new documentary?”

The question prompted Kardashian’s BFF Malika Haqq to note, “I wanted to get his ass.”

ICYMI, in Untold: The Death & Life of Lamar Odom, Odom alleges he only married Kardashian to further his own fame and downplays his ex-wife’s role in ensuring his health and safety in the wake of his near-fatal overdose in 2015—claims that struck deep for Kardashian.

“I don’t need you to thank me, just don’t talk about me,” she said during a confessional during the Kardashians episode. “Don’t discredit me.”

As Kardashian—mom to kids True, 8, and Tatum, 4, with ex Tristan Thompson—put it, “The bridge is burned and there’s no more rope to throw over, so he is dead to me.”

More than anything, the fact Kardashian willingly participated in the documentary, only to have her ex make disparaging comments was the bitter-tasting icing on the cake.

“I’m watching the documentary and I’m like, ‘Wait, I took time out of my life to sit here and do a favor for you,’” the True American founder said in the confessional. “I’m not being paid. I’m doing this ‘cause I’m trying to be nice. And there’s so many things that I just found really distasteful.”

Among them, “saying that you weren’t really in love with me,” Kardashian explained. “You married me just so you could be more popular in pop culture, all these things, which is so weird. Even if that is your truth, why don’t you say it without me being on the other side of these interviews? Like, I just thought that was really, really lame.”

For his part, Odom—dad to three with ex Liza Morales—has since pointed out it was one of his children who specifically alleged he only married Kardashian for the fame, though in Untold he admits to wanting to marry her after seeing the reality star’s family “lifestyle.”

Nevertheless, Kardashian said she has no more patience for such energy.

“This just solidifies what liars, con artists, cheats, thieves, trolls that men are,” she said. “And I just don’t want to be involved. Play with yourself, don’t play with me.”

Hackers exploiting ScreenConnect plugin flaw to spread malware across hundreds of PCs

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

ConnectWise disables third-party extensions in 400 environments as bughunters uncover auth hole may let crims bypass MFA

Hackers have used a dodgy third-party ScreenConnect extension to push malware onto at least 288 machines across 48 organizations, prompting ConnectWise to disable a handful of plugins in hundreds of customer environments.

Security outfit Huntress first raised the alarm in a Reddit post on Wednesday, warning of an active campaign targeting cloud-hosted ScreenConnect instances. At the time, its security operations center was tracking six compromised managed service providers (MSPs) and more than 286 affected endpoints, with the numbers still climbing.

ConnectWise acquired popular remote supporttool ScreenConnect in February, 2015.

Huntress spotted the attacks at around 1747 UTC, when someone began using ScreenConnect's remote command feature to install rogue clients on machines managed by multiple IT service providers. All 288 endpoints were hit within 90 minutes, with roughly a third compromised in the first ten minutes.

Huntress suspects more MSPs may have been compromised, as the attackers appeared to be numbering their victims sequentially, with the numbers suggesting earlier attacks that it hadn't detected.

The attackers deployed a 16-file malware toolkit dubbed SCKit, which installs a separate ScreenConnect client to maintain remote access even if the legitimate connection is shut down. It also contains code designed to spread the infection to IT technicians and potentially other customers under their control.

Huntress has since traced the activity to a possible authentication weakness in RMM+, a third-party extension that lets other management tools issue commands through ScreenConnect without technicians using its console.

Versions 1.0.31 and 1.0.32 of the extension contain a flaw that means installations without a configured access key will accept a blank one. Because RMM+ operates independently of ScreenConnect's normal user accounts and authentication controls, an attacker could potentially execute commands without defeating MFA.

Huntress said the findings matched what its investigators saw during the attacks, with commands reaching machines across several ScreenConnect installations despite no corresponding login activity.

The researchers also confirmed that the attacks affected ScreenConnect versions 26.6.6.9747 and 26.7.6.9783, meaning administrators running the latest releases weren't necessarily safe.

ConnectWise has acknowledged that third-party extensions may have been involved but has stopped short of confirming how the attackers initially gained access.

In a security advisory, the company said it was investigating unauthorized access to certain partner environments and had identified approximately 400 installations containing at least one extension from the RMM+ family.

It has disabled five extensions, including RMM+, RMM Plus, and Labtech Plus, wherever it can do so remotely, and urged customers running their own ScreenConnect servers to follow suit.

ConnectWise stressed that the 400 environments were identified because they had the extensions installed, not because they were necessarily compromised.

The company said: "At this time, we have found no evidence of a compromise of ConnectWise infrastructure or a vulnerability in ScreenConnect itself."

That could give attackers a route from one compromised customer into others managed by the same IT provider. However, Huntress says it has yet to see that part of the attack succeed.

"We have not observed that method to spread work successfully. The console-takeover attempt failed in every recovered host log we have checked so far," the researchers wrote.

Huntress also suspects that some of the malware's C# code was generated using an LLM, pointing to unusually detailed comments explaining what the code is supposed to do. Apparently, even malware authors appreciate a little help with their homework.

Both Huntress and ConnectWise are urging administrators not to investigate suspected infections by remotely connecting through ScreenConnect, since doing so could put the technician's own machine at risk.

Administrators are being advised to use other security tools to investigate affected machines, look for rogue ScreenConnect clients, and check what commands have been run. Any malware left behind will also need to be removed before the machines are brought back online.

Huntress warned on Reddit that some machines were becoming reinfected within minutes of being taken out of isolation because the malware had not been properly removed.

Simply disabling the vulnerable extensions won't necessarily undo the damage, either. As ConnectWise warns, "Disabling extensions and resetting credentials alone may not remove unauthorized software, persistence mechanisms, or access already established on endpoints."

And with the attackers already able to install their own remote access software, kicking them out could prove considerably harder than closing the door they came through. ®

Google Will Now Tell You Exactly How Much It’s Crediting You For Invalid Traffic

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Digital advertisers have always wondered exactly how much of their ad budgets they’re wasting on bots. Now, Google will tell them.

Today, Google Ads announced it’s adding a new feature called the Invalid Traffic Overview Card to its user interface. For the first time, it breaks down the dollar amount a campaign saved from Google’s invalid traffic (IVT) blocking and exactly how much Google is crediting the campaign for bots caught post-billing.

Google’s ad platform has long provided its own IVT-detection tech and credited advertisers for ads served to invalid impressions. This IVT detection always attempts to filter out invalid traffic before the advertiser is billed, a Google spokesperson told AdExchanger. “Or we issue an automatic credit as a billing adjustment on their next invoice when it’s detected later, where appropriate and possible,” they said.

Google’s platform doesn’t just filter out malicious bots as IVT. “Google’s systems analyze all traffic, including agentic activity, to determine if there is genuine interest in a business,” the spokesperson said.

Google aims to filter out any traffic it decides is not serving the advertiser’s business interest before the end of the billing cycle, so there’s no need for a credit, they added. If “ongoing analysis” detects the IVT post-billing cycle, that’s when a credit gets applied.

The credit is equal to the ad spend. “So, if $100 of spend was deemed as invalid traffic and detected after the end of the billing cycle, then a credit is issued for that $100,” the Google spokesperson said. “These credits are applied automatically as a billing adjustment that reduces the amount owed on their next invoice.”

And there are no restrictions on what ad inventory these credits can be applied to, the spokesperson said.

Third-party IVT-detection platforms likewise attempt to credit advertisers for wasted spend, although naturally, Google has direct insight into what’s happening on its platform and can more easily catch IVT before the advertiser gets billed for it.

And Google says recent AI-driven enhancements have improved its IVT detection methods. For example, Google says it saw a 40% reduction in ads served to IVT between 2023 and 2024.

But, in the past, advertisers grappled with a lack of transparency into how they were compensated for ads that were inevitably served to bots. Google heard those complaints, hence why it’s rolling out this feature, the spokesperson said.

The timing also makes sense because IVT and bot traffic continues to grow as a share of online activity. Recent research from cybersecurity group Thales found that automated traffic has eclipsed human traffic, accounting for 53% of online activity this year and 51% last year.

And, according to ad verification platform DoubleVerify, the growing popularity of generative AI and a related uptick in AI scraping activity resulted in IVT increasing by 86% year over year in late 2024.

No one wants to serve ads if doing so has no chance of leading to a purchase or conversion. Now advertisers will have a better idea of just how many bots are out there and how much money they’re saving by not advertising to them.

Tim and Chris Vanderhook explain why Viant bought Waymark and how it fits into their ambitious plans for CTV.

Schibsted is working with Nodals AI, a company that creates custom ad optimization algorithms for publishers, to court brands who’ve been wooed by Meta’s own optimization tools.

AI is taking over the media and tech industries in the name of simplification, and marketers are pining for provable campaign outcomes, including from TV. These shifts are also causing significant change in Agency Holdco Land, according to Angela Steele, the first chief client officer for WPP Media US.

No major retail media player has taken as circumspect an approach to ad revenue as Shopify. But the company has recently taken its next important step with a product called Campaign Autopilot.

OpenAI’s head of monetization, Benji Shomair makes the case for ads as a way to bring ChatGPT to more people, with user trust taking priority over advertiser demands.

In Q3, The Trade Desk beat the admittedly pessimistic revenue forecast it set for itself earlier this year. But growth has stalled.

Don’t Let A Tool Lead Your AI Search Strategy – Own Your Data Instead via @sejournal, @TaylorDanRW

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Keep your AI search strategy independent of unstable tools by owning your prompts and raw data, so vendor changes don't erase your measurement history.

One of the best things about the capabilities of AI is how easily it turns complex, multi-source data into dashboards anyone can understand.

Couple this with the opportunity of creating new tools and platforms for the AI search space, and you end up with this culmination of AI search tools and the need to fill a marketing channel void. The risk is that we reach for dashboards for comfort, especially when we are facing unknowns.

Marketing leadership loves the comfort of a software dashboard.

Search has always operated on a predictable, standardized framework when it comes to tools and platforms. You license an enterprise suite, import your target queries, track deterministic positions from one to one hundred and build quarterly reports that stakeholders can understand at a glance.

When generative search emerged, we naturally sought that same software-first playbook. Marketers looked for a platform to define what AI brand visibility should look like.

When you let software lead your AI strategy, you anchor your organization to an immature, volatile software market. And which tool do you choose with so many new offerings?

I researched Product Hunt, G2, and LinkedIn, and found 194 tools that have launched over the past two years that focus on aiding brands to improve their AI visibility.

Looking at the AI platform landscape, the number of tools and the speed at which they are being launched creates a false illusion of category maturity. Breaking down the 194 tools from this sample across the last two to three years highlights that a little over 60% of them are lightweight bootstrap experiments, side projects, and solo founder builds. The mortality rate of a startup mindset is running between 55% and 60%. This does not include the ones I have not been able to identify and track that do not have a presence on these platforms. Every month, many tools currently switch off APIs, stop renewing domain names, or pivot into unrelated generative wrapper apps.

The main driver of this is the impact of cost. In traditional search monitoring, scraping for our own tracking purposes has been relatively stable and has only become more volatile in recent years. But pinging a search engine and parsing HTML markup costs a fraction of what it takes to fire complex multi-turn prompt matrices when you have live web browsing enabled.

Then, factor in how Google is actively working to combat this practice, meaning that you have to continue reinvesting and adjusting how your stack works to provide the same service. Inference calls, headless browsers, anti-bot bypass proxies, and context tokens all add up relentlessly, especially as your business becomes more successful and scales.

For a self-serve tool charging $100 a month, running comprehensive weekly prompt variations for a large enough sample across a client’s core product catalog, margins are non-existent. The moment an underlying model provider updates its inference pricing, tightens its rate limits, or changes its interface, these projects really show how fragile they are and break down economically and operationally.

See also: AI Visibility Isn’t A Sham – Most Ways Of Measuring It Are

Another trap with allowing a tool to lead your AI strategy is approaching it the same way you would a vendor. Tools often come with multi-year or multi-term contracts. Once that contract is up, or if a better offer comes along from another provider, you will want to move away. It is often assumed that you can just export the data and migrate to the next platform.

We know from traditional SEO tooling that everything is measured slightly differently. While they have become relatively homogenized over the years, this is still a very immature marketplace. There is no standard or uniform underlying measurement of what AI visibility actually means. So when we swap one AI visibility tool for another, it does not mean that we are swapping like for like. It means we are swapping divergent philosophies of measurement and meaning.

Because there is no universally accepted standard for measuring AI and conversational output, every tool invents its own proprietary synthetic index that it also markets as being its main differentiator and USP.

See also: The AI Search Metrics I Use To Track Client Pipelines

From my experience and industry sentiment across social media, there is substantial executive pressure to track AI, meaning these tools are filling an inherent need created within organizations. They will generate an initial audit, take a screenshot of a brand mention for a board deck, and then face the daily reality of stochastic noise. Sources cited inside generative responses can suffer huge amounts of citation drift with high levels of variance.

This occurs without any changes made to the brand’s website or competitors. It is simply the model experiencing variation. Approaching this the same way as a rank tracker means you would spend hours investigating phantom drops and random gains caused by inference temperature shifts or model hallucinations.

Letting a tool dictate the metrics inevitably forces teams and management to track tool idiosyncrasies rather than business fundamentals.

See also: Your AI Visibility Tracker Is Quietly Breaking Your Analytics And Your Strategy

Software cannot be the anchor of your AI search strategy. Strategic stability comes from decoupling your execution from specific vendors. This is easier said than done, and third-party tools do offer an easier path without needing to engineer your own solutions. However, there are core elements you need to take ownership of.

You need to own the query matrix internally and not let a third-party platform decide which queries matter. You need to form your own internal stacks of prompts based on customer intent and categories, high-value comparative queries, and category-defining questions. You need to store this query set in internal documentation belonging to your organization, independent of any external subscription.

You also need to own experimental prompt stacks, brand prompt stacks, and other core elements so that you can track variation across a set and start to measure your own citation drift rates. This is not the same approach as traditional rank tracking.

Try to find external platforms that offer raw webhook exports, BigQuery connectors, or standardized CSVs. You want to extract all response text, timestamp data, source URLs, and prompt inputs directly into your own data infrastructure. If your vendor decides to change its calculation logic, your historical raw observations will remain intact.

Importantly, you need to recognize visibility as an outcome and not an input. A tracking dashboard does not generate any authority.

LLMs do not reference brands because the company bought visibility software. In my experience, they reference brands that possess dense authoritative entity definitions, unambiguous architectures and schemas and active digital footprints.

The software landscape for conversational and generative search will take years to reach maturity. Consolidations will accelerate, venture capital subsidies will dry up, and the vast majority of tools will vanish. Do not construct your executive roadmap around the features of an unstable tool category.

25 Years of Bilibo: The Toy That Lets Imagination Lead

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

With two round eyes and a curving grin, Bilibo looks like a character waiting for its story to begin. Designed by Swiss designer Alex Hochstrasser, the sculptural shell is an open-ended toy that can become anything from a seat or boat to a helmet or drum. Twenty-five years after its 2001 debut, the toy remains remarkably unchanged, a testament to the enduring appeal of a design that leaves almost everything to the imagination.

To mark the milestone, Moluk, the Zurich-based toy company Hochstrasser runs with his sister Doris, an architect, is introducing Bilibo Midi, a smaller shell for toddlers, and Bilibo Dots, a set of colorful counters that translate the original’s open-ended approach into a new form.

Both additions build on the premise that has carried Bilibo into homes, classrooms, and museum collections around the world—and continues to anchor Moluk’s entire catalog: offer a pared-back object and leave the rest to the imagination.

Hochstrasser’s starting point for Bilibo was what he saw on store shelves at the turn of the millennium. “I was struck by how rigidly most mainstream toys were categorized by age and gender, giving them an expiration date long before they wore out,” he says. “There was also a flood of licensed toys, with the story already written. I wanted to create something more universal and inclusive that could grow with children and leave them in charge of the story.”

Alex Hochstrasser’s sketches for the first Bilibo. Illustration courtesy of Moluk.

Firsthand observation of play and repeated rounds of testing shaped the toy, along with input from child-development experts. “The final step in the design belongs to the children,” Hochstrasser explains. “They become the co-inventors, deciding what the object is and what it can do.”

Early studies drew on archetypal forms like eggs and turtle shells. “What interested me was how a hollow hemisphere’s behavior changed simply by turning it over,” Hochstrasser explains. “Resting on its rim, it was stable, a hill to climb or a shelter for things underneath. On its rounded base, it became dynamic, a vessel that could rock and spin.”

The shell’s threefold symmetry allows two identical pieces to lock together into a cheerful sphere. The two openings read as eyes, lending the object personality and a sense of direction—but they also work as peepholes, anchor points for a pull rope, and spouts for pouring water.

“Each detail had to contribute to both its character and its possibilities for play,” he says. “I wanted the form to feel friendly and familiar, yet odd enough to make children curious.”

Bilibo appeared in ‘Century of the Child’ at MoMA in 2012. Photography courtesy of Moluk.

The deceptively simple design soon attracted attention beyond the toy industry, earning a Design Distinction in I.D. Magazine’s 2002 Annual Design Review, an honor the first Apple iPod also received that year. Bilibo went on to appear in MoMA’s Century of the Child: Growing by Design, 1900–2000 in 2012 and enter the permanent museum collections in Zurich, Brussels, and Paris.

Bilibo Midi brings a lighter, more compact shell to younger children, with room for a 2- or 3-year-old to sit and spin. It’s also easy to carry, even when filled with sand or water.

Bilibo Midi toys as stepping stones. Photography courtesy of Moluk.

“A modest change in size can make a big difference to how independently children use an object,” Hochstrasser notes. “When we tested Midi, it was striking how naturally toddlers picked the shells up or used them as stepping stones—no help needed.”

Alongside the smaller shell, Moluk introduced Bilibo Dots, a set of 36 counters, each carrying the original’s curved profile and two eyes in miniature. They can be sorted, counted, threaded, or tossed inside the original Bilibo shell to rattle around.

Bilibo Dots. Photography courtesy of Moluk.

“I see all MOLUK toys as a family of building blocks for play, designed to work together so each new addition opens up possibilities for the others,” Hochstrasser says. “Put a handful of Dots in a Midi, and it’s a treasure chest.”

The Classic Bilibo also comes in four special-edition colors for 2026–2027: amber, emerald, graphite, and coral. Aside from new colors and a 2021 retooling that made it lighter and stronger, the original has retained its form for a quarter century. Children who grew up with Bilibo are now watching a new generation discover it.

“Longevity depends on both the toy surviving and children continuing to find new uses for it,” he says. “Bilibo has no batteries to replace, no screen to update, and no mechanism to break. It’s a simple but distinctive shape that changes character depending on how you position or look at it. It invites rough-and-tumble physical play—rocking, balancing, spinning—and can just as easily become a drum, a boat, or a cradle in a child’s imagination. The object stays the same, but the play it enables keeps changing as the child grows.”

Hayley Arsenault is a writer and editor covering architecture, interiors, and design. Based in New York, she covers materials and manufacturing in the built environment.

Nexo Card data shows its European users spend twice the euro-area average

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

European Nexo Card holders spent about €80 per transaction in 2025, roughly double the euro-area card average. Nearly a third went on travel, eating out and leisure.

A long lunch with a colleague. A wellness class after work. A weekend away, booked from the sofa before bed. The purchases look ordinary. The money behind them does not.

According to Nexo’s new report, Digital assets and the European lifestyle, European Nexo cardholders spent about €80 per transaction in 2025. That is roughly double the euro-area card average of around €38 recorded by the European Central Bank for the second half of 2025. The findings draw on anonymised, aggregated Nexo Card transaction data across the European Economic Area from 1 January to 31 December 2025.

The headline is not that crypto holders spend differently. It is that they spend on the same things as everyone else, with bigger tickets. And they fund it in a way traditional cards cannot.

Nearly a third of all Nexo Card spending went on travel, eating out and leisure. That roughly matches all other retail spending combined once groceries are set aside. Restaurants alone took 88.5% of food and drink spending.

Travel was the card’s second-largest category, at 15.9% of volume. That fits the setting. Europe captured about $2 trillion of the $6.15 trillion spent on leisure travel worldwide in 2025, according to the World Travel & Tourism Council.

And the card is not reserved for special occasions. For more than half of cardholders, it also paid for the weekly grocery shop.

Compared with 2024, spending on the Nexo Card rose fastest in the categories that shape a lifestyle:

The Nexo Card works in two modes. In credit mode, clients borrow against holdings such as Bitcoin and Ether instead of selling them. In debit mode, the card converts digital assets at the point of sale.

When clients pay directly with crypto, stablecoins dominate. 72.7% of that spending goes through assets designed to hold a steady value, although that stability is not guaranteed.

“What the data shows is a European consumer who spends on everyday categories, with higher ticket sizes,” said Yasen Yankov, Head of Product Management at Nexo. “We see digital asset wealth, made spendable by tools like the Nexo Card, becoming a new driver of consumption.”

The spending sits on top of a widening base of ownership. In early 2026, about one in five respondents in the Netherlands and one in six in Germany said they owned crypto assets, according to the Adan and Ipsos 2026 Barometer. In France, 53% of holders had already paid for something with crypto, most often by card.

The missing piece, Nexo argues, has been infrastructure: tools that let people use their assets without selling them, turning stored wealth into everyday spending.

Alongside the report, Nexo announced that its card is now available in several overseas territories and autonomous regions of EU countries, many of them popular travel destinations:

Nexo describes its card, which runs on the Mastercard network, as the first crypto card to combine credit and debit functions.

Payments infrastructure rarely makes headlines. People notice the dinner, the flight and the spa day, not the rails underneath. A year of Nexo Card data suggests that for a growing group of Europeans, those rails are now partly built on digital assets.

The Sisters Behind Dannijo Are Making Sports Merch Cool

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

DannijoPro was built on the idea that female fans deserve game-day gear they actually want to wear.

When Knicks frenzy overtook New York City this past summer during the team’s historic championship run, women fans—both long-suffering devotees and bandwagoners alike—went looking for something to wear. And in a city known for its fashion sense and individuality, the same old generic logo shirts and jerseys simply would not do.

DannijoPro answered the call. Seemingly overnight, the brand’s cropped blue satin bombers, bedazzled denim jackets with crochet collars, and artfully embroidered vintage sports tees took over Instagram, with celebrities like Brooke Shields, cool girls, and Ali Brunson, wife of star point guard Jalen Brunson, and Jordyn Woods, who wed center Karl-Anthony Towns shortly after he won the chip, photographed wearing DannijoPro. Knicks royalty Spike Lee even wore a DannijoPro custom jacket courtside at Game 5.

Danielle and Jodie (Danni + Jo, get it?) Snyder, the sisters and co-founders behind the bespoke fanwear company (a sporty spinoff of their original jewelry and fashion brand, Dannijo), say they were just happy to be along for the historic ride. “No one could have predicted the Knicks were going to go as far as they did, but that was amazing for us,” Jodie says of the win that ended a 53-year title drought. “People loved what we were doing.” Their Knicks finals shirts sold out three times; all 800 satin bombers (priced at $395) sold out, too. Overall, the sisters tell me their sales for 2026 are up 200 percent from last year. “I’ve never seen anything like it in my life,” says Danielle, who, along with Jodie, lived in NYC for over a decade and visited during the finals. “The energy was electric and there was such community. People were buying merch because it was so much more than a garment. It’s a piece of history. It’s a relic now.”

The Knicks run may have fueled their virality, but DannijoPro’s popularity also stems from the fact that it is serving an audience that has been ignored for too long by companies that thought that, when designing sports merch for women, it was enough to “pink it and shrink it.” And although 75 percent of women identify as fans of one or more sports, and women’s sports merch is estimated to be a $4 billion market, still a quarter of female fans say they can’t find styles they like. “It’s crazy that we’re in 2026 and you can go to a game and not find anything you want to buy as a female fan,” Jodie says.

Three months after the Knicks took home the trophy, I reach the sisters as they are gearing up for the launch of their new NBA collection for the 2026-27 season and a number of other new partnerships that have come in the wake of their viral turn, including one rolling out this month with their hometown team, the Jacksonville Jaguars—DannijoPro’s first NFL collab. Danielle is in the brand’s atelier in Tiburon in Marin County, California; as we chat on Zoom, I see people hand-stitching various garments in the background. Jodie joins the call from her car in Jacksonville, Florida, where she heads up the company’s retail and business strategy.

The sisters, who are three years apart, grew up as close as could be in Jacksonville. They’ve been creative, crafty, and entrepreneurial from a young age. Their mom, a mental health counselor, didn’t have pierced ears, but she did have a killer clip-on earring collection, and the girls were obsessed with it. In middle school, Jodie took an interest in making jewelry of her own, and Danielle, wanting to do everything her big sister did, followed right along.

Their dad is a cardiologist, and they taught themselves how to do wire work using his medical tools. Soon, they were collaborating on intricately beaded designs and selling them in local stores. “We’d see kids around school wearing our handmade designs and they didn’t even know it was us making them,” Danielle says. “What we were doing was really fresh and cool. Girls and women could take our accessories and update a white tee and jeans, or anything basic, and make it their own. That was our first taste of using style to individualize and to tell stories and we caught the bug.”

They got their big break a few years later when Danielle was in middle school. Neiman Marcus bought 150 of their turquoise nugget necklaces. “I remember staying up until 3 or 4 in the morning cranking them out,” Danielle says. “And I had an exam the next morning.” The Snyders continued to balance school and their design ambitions. Years later, when Jodie was at the University of Florida studying business and public relations and Danielle at Vanderbilt, where she majored in psychology and Spanish, Jodie would fly up and they would do trunk shows at sororities on campus. “We were always making stuff for wherever we were in life,” Jodie explains. “And everything we do is rooted in sisterhood and wanting to be together, wanting to express ourselves,” Danielle adds.

Jodie graduated from college in 2004 and moved to New York City, with Danielle following in 2007. Dannijo was founded the following year and, like the Snyders’ childhood company, quickly became known for its statement jewelry pieces. Their collection was sold in Bergdorf Goodman and Henri Bendel; characters on Gossip Girl and anchors on the Today show wore their necklaces; Beyoncé and Kim Kardashian did too (purchasing them themselves). “I remember the saleswoman calling us and saying, ‘You’re never going to believe who bought the Ruth Celia necklace,’” Danielle says. It was Queen B, who went on to wear it at Coachella, courtside at NBA games, and on a trip with her husband, Jay-Z, in Europe. “So we started getting press before we even really knew what PR was.”

They soon expanded into fashion, designing the “perfect silk slip dress” modeled after Kate Moss in the ’90s. When Jodie’s daughter was 3, she went to a tie-dye party and the next thing they knew, Dannijo was selling tie-dyed hoodies. They styled it over a slip dress and it went viral. “We sold thousands of tie-dye hoodies—it was insane,” Jodie says. “Basically, we just made things that we wanted to wear.” Collabs with Starbucks, Mercedes-Benz, and Swarovski followed.

In 2020, as the pandemic took hold in NYC, the sisters, like many residents, decamped for other parts of the country. Jodie moved back to Florida, while Danielle moved to the Bay Area. The move across the country was hard on Danielle, who was suffering from postpartum depression. “I was lost, lonely, and I didn’t have community,” she notes.

One day, her husband came home with Golden State tickets and told her they were going out. “That first Warriors game changed my life,” Danielle explains. “It changed my perspective. It got me excited again—I was excited to root for a team and to be a part of something.” They started attending games regularly and Danielle began creating her own designs to wear. “I started going through my husband’s closet and cutting up all of his old jerseys and making them into outfits for me,” she says. “My fix is always to be creative, so I was using my hands, doing stitch work, adding crystals, what have you.” Other female fans at the games began taking notice of her creative designs and asking where she had bought them. “Everyone was noticing because there was just nothing like it out there, so I called Jodie and I said, ‘This is really big.’” Soon, Dannijo’s sporty sister brand, DannijoPro, was born.

Launching a company focused on women fans was a “no-brainer,” Danielle says. “When I started making myself stuff to wear, that’s when I noticed what a crazy white space it was. Sports have never been more popular, but the product just had not caught up to that reality.” It took a year and a half to get an NBA license, but once they did, the first DannijoPro collection, which rolled out in 2024 at the Chase Center (where the Warriors play), quickly sold out.

As ever, the sisters are their own inspiration. Jodie has two children and Danielle has three. “Our worlds have changed,” Jodie says. “We’re both in T-shirts and sweatshirts; we’re not getting dressed up every day anymore.” As such, the DannijoPro items they make aren’t just for game day. They are for the 70 percent of women fans who say they’ve bought sports merch, while only 51 percent have bought tickets to attend an event. “We wanted to create items that people want to wear on and off the court or field,” Jodie says. “It's about making flattering silhouettes and clothes we all want to wear, and then applying them to the teams that we’re excited about.”

DannijoPro has three main products: officially licensed merch complete with the signature touches the designers have come to be known for, like crochet collars, hand-stitching, painting, and crystal work; “blanks” that can be customized with the help of its in-house artists (most orders come via DM); and vintage pieces that are reworked by hand.

As Jodie talks, Danielle runs around the atelier pulling out products from their new collections to show me. In August, they launched designs they made for avatars in the NBA 2K game. In September, they debuted a collab with Roger Federer’s Laver Cup tennis tournament. Their new NBA collection, called “Game Day 2.0,” rolled out last month, while their Jacksonville Jaguars line will go on sale later in November. Future partnerships with other teams and leagues are in the works.

The sisters see their new company as “so much bigger than a fashion brand,” Danielle says. “We are reimagining fandom through a culture lens, and it’s about people. We’re living in a world of AI where everything’s like a bot. For us, it’s like, how do we bring really cool dynamic experiences in real life to people and create connections and community?”

“We think women fans should be celebrated in that there are products out there that speak to us,” Jodie adds. “It’s more than merch. I think it stands for so many other things. We can’t just accept that it’s okay to just slap logos on things, or pink it and shrink it, and women are just going to go with that. And at the end of the day, all these companies forget, but women have the buying power.”

Four video tracks and a doughnut shop – how Dispatch got its start

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Before it was an animated smash, Dispatch was a live-action browser experiment with a hard technical limit and one very durable doughnut scene. AdHoc’s founders explain the pivot.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A year ago, Dispatch released a game that surprised many people, including me. It's a superhero game about managing reformed villains from behind a desk, launched on PC and PlayStation 5. It has since sold five million copies, and a mobile version arrives on iOS and Android on 9 December, following October's Dispatch anniversary Deluxe Edition.

Dispatch was made in Unreal Engine and stars Breaking Bad's Aaron Paul as former hero Robert Robertson, alongside Jeffrey Wright, Laura Bailey, and members of the Critical Role cast. It's an animated, episodic workplace comedy that plays like a TV series, but it wasn't always a game, and that's perhaps why it feels so unique. AdHoc was founded in Los Angeles in 2018 by Telltale Games alumni who planned to make interactive experiences with real actors on screen, and Dispatch began that way.

I asked co-founders Nick Herman and Dennis Lenart, the studio's COO and CTO and both co-creative directors, what survived the jump from live-action series to animated game, and what had to change.

One piece of the original script made it through every draft: the doughnut shop scene in episode two. Lenart says it was "one of the pieces from the early script that just kind of kept hanging around through every draft all the way from live action into the animated game".

The set-up was different back then. You watched a camera feed as the hero Invisigal dealt with a thief, and your job was to feed her the information she needed to succeed. In the live-action version, that meant switching cameras and pulling information from what each camera showed. Herman says the scene was "purely built around the technology" AdHoc was using to create its interactive live action. It ran in a browser and had hard limits, including, as Herman recalls, “I think it was like four simultaneous tracks of video”.

The team turned that restriction into a mechanic. Watch the alley behind the shop, and you couldn't see what was happening inside the front. "By choosing where you were going to give your attention, the game would adjust, and you'd learn different things based on where you were looking," says Herman, calling it the perfect place to test the idea.

Because they'd spent so long on it, they carried it forward. In the game, Lenart says, they wove in mechanics from the final design, such as hacking, "to make it feel a little bit more like Robert was helping out Invisigal".

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

The original story was a mentorship, centred on the one-on-one relationship between Robert and Invisigal. Pivoting it into a game changed that. AdHoc wanted a dispatch mechanic, with the player sending heroes out on calls, and that needs a roster. "So the Z-Team was created to support the gameplay," Lenart says, "which then informed the story, and then changed the overall arc of the season, which brought a whole team into it as opposed to just a one-on-one mentorship with Invisigal."

It's a neat illustration of how the studio works. "Everything at AdHoc starts with story," says Herman, contrasting that with studios that pick a genre first, polish the mechanics and the game-feel, and only later ask what the story is. AdHoc begins with the world, the characters and who the player is, then asks what that means for gameplay. "Oh, well, you're a superhero dispatcher, so maybe we should have some sort of dispatching mechanic, right?"

Once there's a script, it moves on to storyboards, animatics and animation. That storyboard stage is now part of the product: the anniversary Deluxe Edition update adds Storyboard Mode, which plays the whole game through its original animatics. It's a new mode that offers genuine insight into how a scene was planned, scripted and animated.

The move to animation was driven by budget constraints, to an extent, because they could do more with a little extra, and it also changed the game's scale, given Dispatch's live-action origins; Lenart says the team knew they were "severely limited from a budget standpoint compared to big-budget superhero tentpole movies". For budgetary reasons, the story was largely set in an office with a small cast.

Animation flipped that constraint and aligned with trends like Mixtape's hand-made animation, giving the team room to grow and aim higher within the new art form. "We're able to have a giant action scene because it doesn't cost that much more than having a bunch of characters walking around talking," Lenart explains. That let AdHoc expand the moments that deserved spectacle: "Let's really go all out and try to compete with some of the other best-in-class versions of these scenes in animation."

Ask whether players have done anything that made AdHoc rethink how interactive stories should work, and Herman is refreshingly honest. “I don’t know that there have been any major epiphanies,” he says, before steering to a different answer: launching Dispatch “was a labour of love”, the culmination of a lot of work distilled into one experience the team felt was exciting and high quality.

He adds: "The success of the game has just really injected a lot of confidence into us and other developers making similar types of games. For a long time, there hadn't been a really clear or recent example of something like this reaching a level of success that a developer could go out and raise funding for."

He's proud of who found it, too. Dispatch "was able to pierce a new audience who perhaps thought gaming wasn't for them". Lenart agrees, saying the success feels like "motivation to keep making these types of games and expand our possibilities even more".

The Dispatch Deluxe Edition is out now on PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X, and Nintendo Switch / Switch 2, and $10.99 digital upgrade path for existing owners

Former Crescent-Stars midfielder Sergen Yalcin takes over and would immediately endear himself to the fans if he can somehow avoid relegation to League B, with Turkey needing to beat Belgium by four goals, then overcome France on Sunday while hoping tonight's opponents lose to Italy.

Centre back Abdulkerim Badarkci is suspended and Juventus forward Kenan Yildiz remains sidelined through injury, so Arda Guler will once again be responsible for carrying most of Turkey's attacking threat as he aims to carry his good form with Real Madrid onto the international stage.

Belgium start the international window just one point behind second-place Italy, who they face on Sunday, so a victory in Izmir would keep their fate in their own hands when they take on the Azzurri in Brussels.

The Red Devils need to finish in the top two of Group A1 to qualify for the quarter-finals and a pair of wins this week would secure their place in the last eight.

Manager Mark van Bommel has been boosted by the return of Manchester City winger Jeremy Doku, who missed the previous Nations League window through injury. He is expected to start against Turkey, despite lively performances by wide men Mika Godts and Dodi Lukebakio in September and October.

The hosts will be out to impress their new manager, but that may not be enough.

Astronomers stunned after James Webb Space Telescope discovers the earliest pair of black hole-powered quasars

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"There is no definitive theoretical explanation for how a single one of these giant quasars exists so early in the age of our universe, much less two."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Using the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST), astronomers have discovered the oldest pair of supermassive black hole-powered quasars ever seen. There's just one problem: they shouldn't exist!

The compact quasar pair, spotted with the aid of the Gemini North telescope, has been designated J1602+4228AB and nicknamed Nāpōwawāʻenakaulua, which means "Two celestial bodies of fathomless powerful darkness in the brilliance of heat reincarnating energy into the universe." The compact quasar pair is seen as it was 900 million years after the Big Bang at a time when such supermassive black holes should not have had time to grow to such tremendous masses.

Nāpōwawāʻenakaulua isn't just the oldest and most distant quasar pairing ever seen. There is just 18,100 light-years, or roughly one-fifth the diameter of the Milky Way, between the supermassive black holes, which have masses of 1.5 billion and 400 million times that of the sun. That makes Nāpōwawāʻenakaulua the most compact quasar pair found during a period of the universe's history known as Cosmic Dawn, shortly after the birth of the first stars, galaxies, and black holes.

"There is no definitive theoretical explanation for how a single one of these giant quasars exists so early in the age of our universe, much less two," research team leader Silvia Onorato of the NOIRLab said in a statement.

"We observed Nāpōwawāʻenakaulua at a time when the universe was only a thirteenth of its age and, astonishingly, its supermassive black holes are just as massive as those we see today."

Quasars occur when the supermassive black holes that sit at the heart of large galaxies are surrounded by a flattened cloud of swirling gas and dust called an "accretion disk," from which they feed or "accrete" matter. The immense gravity of the central black hole causes friction, heating the accretion disk and causing it to glow, often brighter than the combined light of every star in the surrounding galaxy. We see that glow as a quasar.

Finding such objects in the recent cosmic history of the 13.8-billion-year-old universe isn't troubling, though finding compact pairs of them is still extremely rare. Such pairs should be common because the merger of galaxies that brings together supermassive black holes is frequent. That should lead to an abundance of pairs of quasars with a separation less than the typical size of a galaxy. There are three reasons this has proven tricky. Firstly, the "quasar phase" of a supermassive black hole is relatively short on cosmic terms. Secondly, even relatively close quasar pairs are very far away, making them faint and hard to detect with ground-based telescopes. Finally, at such great distances and with such a small separation between the two supermassive black holes, the individual members of a quasar pair are hard to individually resolve.

Yet, astronomers do find quasar pairs. Sometimes at distances and times that they would probably rather not, because it challenges everything we know about black hole growth.

The trouble arises before the universe was 1 billion years. That is because, according to current models, black holes should need at least 1 billion years to feed and merge enough to reach masses millions or billions of times that of the sun.

Yet, since it began operations in 2022, the JWST has been routinely discovering supermassive black holes powering quasars as early as 400 million years after the Big Bang. If that wasn't confusing enough, the discovery of Nāpōwawāʻenakaulua, the third quasar pair seen at Cosmic Dawn, could really break current models.

Nāpōwawāʻenakaulua was uncovered in data from the JWST's Aether survey, which is conducting a census of objects in the early universe. Using JWST’s NIRSpec (Near-Infrared Spectrograph Integral Field Unit) instrument, the survey has uncovered 200 quasars, just under half of all currently known early universe quasars. It was while browsing the Aether dataset that the team discovered that quasar J1602+4228A is not alone, it had a companion now designated J1602+4228B.

Resolving these two quasars should have been nearly impossible from the ground. But this was possible for Gemini North thanks to perfect timing and perfect weather conditions over Maunakea, the dormant volcano on the island of Hawai'i where the telescope is located.

A definite scientific feat, Nāpōwawāʻenakaulua remains a mystery for now, but it could actually help scientists better understand how galaxies were coming together during Cosmic Dawn, a vital stage in the evolution of the universe.

"Nāpōwawāʻenakaulua is a really unique environment," Onorato said. "It's a laboratory for astronomers to study how the very first massive galaxies and their central black holes assembled together through direct galaxy interactions during these early times."

The team's results were published on Tuesday (Nov. 10) The Astrophysical Journal Letters.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.
