Past and Prologue: Burberry’s Daniel Lee is Bringing Fashion—and His Extended Family—Home.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Daniel Lee must be considering the notion of home a lot these days. Not just his 17th-century country house in his native Yorkshire, which he bought last year, but his London place, on a leafy 19th-century square, where he has lived for the last five years. Lee, Burberry’s chief creative officer, is enjoying a reprieve from having repair professionals in—the AC has been on the blink, the underfloor heating went awry, and he has already endured one soul-destroying flood. On this hot July morning, he has sequestered himself in the basement, decorated with a beige sectional sofa and a Gary Hume painting, Greenness Is a Kind of Grief. The room looks onto the garden, and Lee has taken to lying out on a lounger; by adjusting its angle and that of the television, he can watch soccer from outside.

“My social life is watching the match on TV; it’s a bit sad, isn’t it?” Lee says in a soft voice accented by dry humor. “I love all sport—Wimbledon’s great—but I prefer the energy of a football match to a tennis match. Tennis is very polite. People lose themselves at football—it’s like a cult, isn’t it?” I didn’t have Lee down as a sports fanatic, but he seems to revere footballers as much as the likes of Skepta and Olivia Colman, whom he loves collaborating with. (Lee supports Arsenal.) There’s also his daily 8 a.m. gym sessions before work. “The bench press is nice, because you can just lie there,” he says, laughing. “I don’t love cardio anymore—it hurts my knees.”

From left: Edie Campbell wears silk poplin trench coat and sequined georgette dress. Alva Claire wears map-print nylon sateen trench coat. Clothes and accessories (throughout), Burberry.

Lee, 40, tells me this as we sit in his garden, the first of our several meetings. In person, Lee is candid, funny, and thoughtful. “Daniel appreciates realness—and openness,” says Skepta. “And he has the best eye. I’d pretty much wear everything he makes.”

Appointed Burberry’s chief creative officer in 2022, Lee has taken the 170-year-old British brand from his highly conceptual debut, all electric-blue blanket checks and graphic red-rose prints, to his more recent collections, widely praised as much for their cool, considered functionality as for the cut and thrust of their forward direction. (Lee has also seen the brand reach pretax profits of over $65 million in 2026, up from an almost $88 million loss last year.) He came to Burberry after three years at Bottega Veneta—an era that flared up like a firework thanks to the success of the Cassette bag and Lido sandals, with Lee quitting before the spark extinguished itself. Before that: six years with Phoebe Philo at Celine, eventually becoming her design director; and before that Donna Karan in New York, following internships at Maison Margiela and Nicolas Ghesquière–era Balenciaga after his graduation from Central Saint Martins.

While the CV reads like a conceptualist designer’s dream, Lee’s Burberry role may be the most reflective of who he really is: pragmatic, unpretentious, driven by a sense of purpose. For his friend Rosie Huntington-Whiteley, who has known him since Bottega Veneta, Lee the person and the designer are one and the same. “The reason I feel so at ease in Daniel’s company is that he is grounded and down-to-earth,” she says. “He has this incredible talent and work ethic, yet there’s no pretense with him, no drama.”

From left: Romeo Beckham wears silk shirt, silk scarf, leather trousers, belt, and biker boots. Simone Ashley wears embellished satin dress, leather shoes, and frame bag. Paapa Essiedu wears nylon sateen trench coat, silk shirt, wool trousers, and leather shoes.

When he started at Burberry, Lee says, “it took me a minute to adjust. What I did in the past was about the dream [of fashion], and it was the accessories that were the focus. The clothes here are still the dream, yet they’re wearable and useful, not just for a show. Designing Burberry makes you question and consider everything.” He sees himself as “a making designer,” he says. “I enjoy being in the moment and working with instinct, with craft, with my hands—the physicality of that.”

Lee’s love of making comes from his family—his father Ian and mother Jenny, his brother Richard and sister Sophie. (Daniel is the eldest.) Ian was a mechanic before he retired, and he now lives in Lee’s Yorkshire home, which is not far from Bradford, where Lee was born and raised. He’s set up a workshop at the back of the house to renovate motorcycles, a lifelong passion, which delights his son to no end—when he and his brother were kids, they would ride the bikes in a field at the back of their house. His mom was the creative one. “She was always making—sewing curtains and baking fancy birthday cakes; she made a Noah’s ark for us, and she even made my aunt, who had a cleaning business, a vacuum cleaner,” Lee says. “It made me think, I just want a job where I can make things.”

Simone wears leather shirt, pleated leather skirt, boots, and bag.

Curtains that Lee’s mom sewed for the family living room now hang in one of the bedrooms at his house in Yorkshire, although “his” is a bit of a misnomer: Lee sees the place as his whole family’s home. The impetus to find somewhere in the area came in the aftermath of his mom’s death just before Christmas 2024. “In those moments,” Lee says, “the creative element of work was soothing—it was like therapy.” He hadn’t even been thinking about getting a place up north, but the undertow of emotion carried him there. His brother and sister-in-law, who live locally, were looking to buy a new home. Lee joined the hunt, and the house he ended up taking was the first and only one he saw. “Before that, I’d always had this quest to live abroad, work abroad, see the world,” he says. “When you’re young, you feel like you’ve got endless time—[then] you realize that, actually, time is finite. My family’s not going to be around forever. I want to spend more time with them.” (One area where Lee would rather keep his private life private is when it comes to dating, though he readily says that he’d like to start his own family: “I would love to have children.”)

His aunt Debbie, his mom’s sister, has moved next door to the 1680s house. It was Debbie who brought Lee to London for the first time when he was 12—they saw the London Eye, took in The Lion King, and ate at the then hip restaurant Sketch. “My aunt was being trendy,” Lee says, laughing affectionately. “She also took me to Harvey Nichols in Leeds. I wasn’t a kid growing up reading Vogue, but looking at the amazing windows at Harvey Nichols and seeing the clothes—that’s when I started to think about fashion.” His grandparents would take him to the Alhambra Theatre in Bradford to see the likes of Chicago (his grandmother’s favorite), which ignited a lifelong love of theater. (On a recent trip to New York, he caught the much-lauded revival of The Rocky Horror Show.)

While Lee’s family has always attended his Burberry runway shows, in the weeks and months after his mom’s death, the closeness deepened. His grandmother came to the show in February 2025 with a photograph of Lee’s mom in her handbag, and it was she who sat with him the night before the presentation as he worked on the last of the fittings. His sister, meanwhile, wore the Bur­berry trench that had been their mom’s. “She wore it to my first show,” Lee recalls.

“One thing about Daniel is that he has never forgotten his roots,” says Lee’s father, “and I think that’s why Yorkshire will always feel like home to him.” That feeling of reconnection is something to which Lee has given a lot of thought. “That’s the real value of the North [for me],” he says. “It gives you a sense of shared ownership of the difficult times, which makes things easier.”

Burberry’s headquarters are on Horseferry Road in London, walking distance from both Buckingham Palace and Tate Britain. Lee’s office and studio are on the seventh floor. He designs Tuesday to Thursday, reserving Monday and Friday for prep days—so the team, he says, can have their weekends free of work. Lee likes to be efficient—the late Virgil Abloh told him that creative directors shouldn’t waste their time emailing, so he doesn’t: He and his team speak constantly via WhatsApp instead.

In one corner of the vast workroom, Burberry archivist Carly Eck has assembled a rail of pieces taken from the 25,000 that the company holds—including the earliest trench, dating from 1917—with an eye to what Lee has responded to: the 1930s Highlander cape, with straps to allow freedom of movement; a motoring overall that Lee riffed on for his fall 2024 collection; and a coat worn by explorer Ernest Shackleton, which Lee also referenced in 2023. Eck and Lee get together at the beginning of every season to help build narratives connecting Burberry past and present. Lee’s intense curiosity has certainly paid unexpected dividends. The ridiculously cute duck hats that he included in his Burberry debut were inspired by drawings in one of the brand’s catalogs circa 1930s, laid out on a table nearby.

“What we do,” Lee says, “is try to provide people with answers about how to live the best possible way. We make clothes to protect people, to make them feel good, ready, sophisticated. I think the fashion is secondary. The design here starts from a place of utility, functionality, practicality—it has to work before it has to be, let’s say, decorative.” His Burberry collections thus far have variously offered blanket-lined parkas, battalion jackets, long kilts, jaunty capelets, and dandyish suiting; what model and equestrian Edie Campbell, who has worked with Burberry since she was 15 (“I still had braces!”), calls “very wearable, but with an edge. It’s joyful, and there’s a lightness and a playfulness to it.” Accessories—the ka-ching! successes for any fashion brand—are on his mind, even if a star hasn’t quite emerged yet.

But if the past is in the room, so is the future. Burberry’s spring 2027 collection, which he showed in September at Chelsea College of Arts—Jodie Turner-Smith, Jonathan Bailey, and Fiona Shaw on the front row; Central Cee on the catwalk—features trenches both diaphanous and etched with embroidery, as well as rendered in leather that’s been lavishly traced with beading; utilitarian cotton drill mixed with sequins; floral blousons and skirts worn with earthy, low-heeled buckled mules; and jewel-like high slingback shoes made a little less precious by being paired with slouchy socks. One thing always on Lee’s mind: making Burberry speak more to women. “The brand is born out of menswear, and the trenches are a very masculine thing,” he says. “We’re always trying to bring more femininity to it.”

Edie wears silk poplin jacket with shearling trim, sequined jersey dress, and leather shoes.

Color is one way into that, and it’s something that Lee has decisively brought to the brand from the very beginning with what he describes as softer, moorland tones; spring 2027, meanwhile, sings with shades of dusky pink, aubergine, duck-egg blue, and a soft-sunny yellow, reflecting the casual, lived-in feel that he wanted to impart on the clothes. Gary Hume, the painter, recalls meeting Lee when they began working on a Burberry project in 2024. Walking around Hume’s studio, they’d touch the art, communing with it. Having now worked with Lee, the artist feels that the designer’s use of color “doesn’t feel like it’s added on—it’s entirely integral to what Daniel does.”

For spring 2027, Lee also worked with the celebrated octogenarian print designer Celia Birtwell, an intrinsic part of the creative explosion in British art and design in the 1960s, along with her husband, designer Ossie Clark, and David Hockney, her close friend. Lee had gone to see the recent Hockney exhibition at the Serpentine Galleries in London—he feels a strong affinity with the Bradford-born artist, who died in June—and a gingham tablecloth that Hockney used as a leitmotif sparked an idea to collaborate with Birtwell, who created a check with a painterly feel to it and some rather charming vintage-wallpaper floral prints. They feel organic and fresh, a million miles away from CAD—or AI. “Celia brings a human hand, an emotional hand, to something,” Lee says, thrilled by what she has done. “We have an incredible archive, but sometimes it’s exciting to see new things.”

From left: Alva wears mohair/silk shawl coat and chevron-knit dress. Romeo wears leather jacket with shearling collar, silk shirt, flocked jeans, and belt.

Lee and I are to meet for the last time at the Regency Café near the Bur­berry headquarters. Founded in 1946, it hasn’t changed much since, still a haven of egg and chips, Formica, and tea so wonderfully strong you could stand a teaspoon up in it. In its own way, the café’s story isn’t so different from Burberry’s: halcyon Britain, yet a Britishness of global fascination. Seated among the Regency regulars are hordes of creatively dressed kids from all around the world Instagramming their full English breakfasts—the very kind of young, style-conscious types who Burberry would want to attract, though not only them, of course. It is an egalitarian brand, as much for the aristo in a beaten-up trench driving a Land Rover as it is for the bloke on the football terraces.

Pride in Britishness is, after all, intrinsic to Burberry. “It stands for heritage and culture, and all the things and all the people that those mean,” says model Alva Claire, and Lee seconds the thought. “We are the most famous British luxury brand,” he says, “but in a way we’re bigger than fashion: We are a British institution. We’re there to celebrate the best of Britain—great creativity and also great locations.” Lee recounts how so many of his Burberry shoots have been deliberately located outside of London—Skye, Somerset, Norfolk, the Lake District—and he readily agrees (while acknowledging the importance of London Fashion Week to Burberry) that it would be great to show somewhere else in the UK. But where? “Chanel did Manchester…,” I say; “Dior did Scotland,” he adds.

Lee on the winding, cobbled streets of Haworth, in West Yorkshire.

Right now, though, Lee is thinking about 170 years’ worth of moments. The anniversary is, he says, “always on our minds. How do we do it justice? We have been ambitious this year.” Indeed: As well as a monthslong campaign photographing 170 people all over the world wearing Burberry, a special exhibition, “The Burberry Trench: Crafting an Icon,” is at the V&A ahead of the company’s funding of the museum’s new costume wing, opening in 2028; another Burberry exhibition, “Ever Forward: The Archive On Stage,” opens this month in Shanghai.

Anyone would be asking themselves what’s left to do—and for Lee, there’s plenty. Top of mind for him, professionally, is transforming the brand’s retail environments for its 413 outposts around the world. Personally? Well, he wouldn’t mind, at some point in his life, living in a hot country. Yet he’s not in any rush. He wants progress to be measured and meaningful; to take it in stride. “I mean,” he says, smiling, “I really feel quite content.”

The Health Benefits of Stair Climbing

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

We’ve all heard the recommendation to “take the stairs” instead of the escalator or elevator. Not only is it an easy way to squeeze more movement into your day, but research has linked stair climbing to better cardiovascular health and longevity. Even the famously long-lived residents of Barbagia, Sardinia—one of the world’s so-called Blue Zones—spend much of their day navigating steep hills and staircases.

But nutritionist Marián García—better known as Boticaria García, author of Mujeres de Hierro (Iron Women)—takes the humble staircase one step further.

According to Garcia, the benefits of climbing stairs are more numerous than many of us realize: the activity can help increase energy expenditure, improve cardiovascular fitness, strengthen muscles and bones, and even blunt post-meal blood sugar spikes—benefits that become especially valuable after 40, when metabolism, muscle mass, and hormone levels begin to shift.

According to García, consistency matters more than intensity. “When I started running, I incorporated stairs on my way home as the finishing touch to my workout,” she writes in Mujeres de Hierro. “Over time, the habit grew, and today I not only seek out stairs wherever I go—whether at work or in hotels—but sometimes I even climb them carrying grocery bags or suitcases.”

You don’t even need to spend hours on the stair climber at the gym to reap the benefits. García’s recommendation is easy to remember: Simply aim to climb three flights of stairs three times a day. The advice comes from a study in which sedentary adults climbed approximately 60 steps—about three flights—at a brisk pace three times a day, three days a week, for six weeks. By the end of the study, participants had significantly improved their cardiovascular fitness.

García recommends aiming for an effort level of around six or seven out of 10. “You should be breathing harder and your legs should know they’re working,” she says. “You don't need to sprint or arrive at the top completely out of breath—but you shouldn’t be climbing while looking at your phone, either.”

Once stair climbing becomes part of your routine, García suggests making two small adjustments.

Take two steps at a time: “Your stride lengthens, and your glutes and legs have to work harder. That muscle contraction also increases the stimulus on the bone,” she explains.

Vary your pace: Alternate between climbing quickly and more slowly rather than maintaining the same rhythm throughout. “Changing the pace increases the demand and prevents your legs from climbing on autopilot,” she explains.

The strategy is based on what’s known as Frost’s mechanostat—the idea that bone adapts to the loads placed upon it. “If bone receives sufficient and varied stimuli, it understands that it needs to stay strong. If we never challenge it, it becomes complacent,” García says.

One of stair climbing’s most surprising benefits has to do with blood sugar. “Stairs are like a mini gym between floors,” García says. “They engage your glutes and legs, get your heart pumping, break up sedentary time, and increase energy expenditure.”

Research has also found that just one minute of stair climbing after a meal can help reduce the post-meal rise in blood glucose. “No, it doesn’t cancel out the pudding for dessert,” García jokes, “but it gets your muscles to remove some of the glucose circulating in your blood.”

Of course, she isn’t suggesting that stair climbing replaces strength training or cardio. Instead, she sees it as something even more valuable: a tiny habit that’s easy to repeat every day. “It’s a small, repeated action that gradually builds up your health savings,” she notes.

Tennessee State Representative-Elect Allie Phillips: ‘Living the Struggle Helps Me Understand the Struggle’

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

It’s pretty remarkable that Allie Phillips answers her own emails, given the mother of two—and newly elected Democratic member of the Tennessee House of Representatives—has been fielding media inquiries nonstop since last week’s midterm elections. In Nashville the weekend after her win, Phillips’s name was still plastered everywhere—from the Honky Tonk Highway entertainment district to the city’s oldest diner to venerable lesbian karaoke institution the Lipstick Lounge.

In an interview conducted over Zoom a few days later, I spoke to the staunch reproductive-rights advocate and self-described “regular, working-class mom” about turning trauma into action, campaigning while pregnant, and hitting the ground running with a full slate of legislative priorities. Read that conversation below.

Vogue: Congratulations! What did election night look like for you?

Allie Phillips: Election night had a pivot in it. I ended up having to leave the watch party for our county party and move over to another location because it was just so full. When I saw the early vote numbers hit, I was about 20% ahead, and within five minutes, a member of the Republican Party in Montgomery County called me and said, “Congratulations, Representative Phillips.” I was like, “None of the precincts have been reported yet,” and he said, “Your opponent would have to take 75% of the Election Day vote, and that’s just not going to happen, so congratulations.” I was like, “I’m going to wait until the last vote is counted, I don’t want to prematurely celebrate.”

I spent the night with amazing organizations like the Equity Alliance Fund, a Black-founded organization that works on expanding Black political engagement, and TIRRC, the Tennessee Immigrant & Refugee Rights Coalition. They contributed to so much of my election-night win—they knocked on over 30,000 doors in my district—so being able to celebrate with them was incredible. When we shifted the watch party to a different location, I stood up on a chair in a restaurant in heels, no podium, no microphone, no media, just me and the people that got me over the finish line, and gave my speech that I wrote. It was probably the most down-to-earth, authentic thing that I think could have happened. I wouldn’t change it for the world, because as stressful as it was to have to pivot in the middle of election night, I did what I needed to do as a leader. It was hectic and chaotic, but we got to celebrate together, and that’s what mattered.

Can you talk about the path that brought you to the Tennessee State House last week?

In 2023, I found out that my pregnancy was incompatible at my anatomy scan. I was almost 20 weeks pregnant, and Tennessee had just recently implemented its near-total abortion ban. Due to my daughter’s conditions, my doctor shared that the longer I stayed pregnant, the worse my health would become. I had a daughter at home already, so I didn’t want to put myself at risk. I had to start looking out-of-state for abortion care, and every state surrounding us is also an abortion-ban state, so we had to go even further.

When you have states like Tennessee and others with abortion bans, you have this influx of out-of-state patients bombarding these clinics that are the closest to them, right? So the wait lists were long. The airfare and travel expenses were costly, so I had to start a GoFundMe online because we couldn’t afford the trip to New York City, where we ended up finding a clinic. I went to that clinic 10 days after my high-risk appointment and found out that my daughter had passed in utero, and my body was not expelling her. The doctor said that because the last confirmed fetal heartbeat was almost two weeks ago, I was now at a greater risk of infections or blood clots or going septic, so essentially, we needed to do the procedure now. Within an hour of finding out that my daughter was gone, I was in a gown going into surgery, and I had to do all that alone. My husband wasn't allowed to be there with me. I’d never been in New York City and didn’t know these doctors from Adam, so that experience was very traumatizing.

Phillips testifying about the lack of reproductive freedom in Tennessee during a Senate Budget Committee hearing in 2024.

When I got back home to Clarksville—after the postpartum black hole that I fell into—I was reached out to by the Center for Reproductive Rights, who asked if I’d be interested in joining a lawsuit against the state of Tennessee that was similar to the one in Texas with [Amanda] Zurawski. I don’t think I’ve said yes to anything quicker in my life. I was like, “Somebody has to do something, because this is atrocious and dehumanizing.” The government made me feel like I was gum under their shoe.

I joined the lawsuit, and now I’m the lead plaintiff in the lawsuit. It’s still ongoing three years later. I have not had the opportunity to testify in a trial because the state keeps moving it, but I knew that would take a while. I knew there wasn’t going to be a decision made by the end of the year, so I went and met with my representative at the time, Jeff Burkhart, and shared with him that I wanted help with the Miley’s Law legislation that I wrote that would give exceptions for fatal fetal anomalies. He said he would help me get it up to the General Assembly, but he never did, and then I got sick and tired of waiting around for him to help me and decided that I would just run against him so I could do it myself.

I had no idea what I was doing. I’d never been involved in politics other than voting, and that was, like, presidential elections. I joined my county party and put my hat in for the race, and I ran a 13-month campaign. We raised over half a million dollars, which is unheard of for a Democrat running in deep red Tennessee for a State House office. I had donations from all 50 states. The news coverage of my race was just insane, and unfortunately we still lost, but I took over 45% of the vote. I was pregnant at the end of that campaign. The first three months of my pregnancy were the most grueling three months of the election, August to November.

Being pregnant in August in Tennessee doesn’t sound easy.

Yeah. It was hot and I was super nauseous and I still had to go out and knock on doors. But I did what I needed to do, and then went off to have my son on April 17, 2025, and was still involved. I was pregnant going to marches and stuff, like, you ain’t stopping me. Then I took a little hiatus for that summer. When I came back to work, I started getting more involved again, and then my opponent [Burkhart] died and there was an appointment to his seat, and so I put my name in for the appointment. Our county commission went through five rounds of voting, ended in a tie between me and Michael Lankford, the Republican, and the mayor gave his deciding vote to Michael Lankford, so Michael Lankford was appointed to the seat. He served this last session, so I ran against him this year. We went from being 45% of the vote in 2024 to taking 55% of the vote this year, which was not what we were anticipating. We thought I might win by a couple of hundred votes, maybe, and I think we took it by about 1,600.

I think it says a lot about the dynamic of voters and where they’re sitting with parties right now, because I can tell you, the majority of those voters were not Democrats. There were independents, there were Republicans, and I think that’s incredible. We really had a message of putting working families first; like, I’m a mom of two, I come to work in Nashville every day, my husband works the night shift because we can’t afford childcare, and we do what we have to do to get by. I’m not some wealthy lawyer or entrepreneur with my own business who’s like, Oh, sure, I can be a lawmaker, too. I’m just a regular working-class mom, and I think people want to see more of that in their government. They saw their families in me. They saw themselves in me. I think that’s what made the difference this time versus in the last election, which was just, Oh, she’s all about abortion. What else is she running on? I was like, “A lot of things!” Nobody wanted to talk about them, though, because everybody wants to talk about my story.

Can you tell me a bit about the district you’re now representing?

I am in District 75, which is the western portion of Clarksville. It’s Montgomery County, and the Tennessee portion of the Fort Campbell military base is in my district. Outside of Clarksville’s city limits, we have a lot of really rural farm areas, and that makes this district really unique because we have so much agriculture, and then you come into Clarksville and it’s kind of urban and suburban, a little bit of both. With it being a military town, people are coming and going all the time, so in a small rural district, you might run for office once and then run again and knock the same doors and meet the same people, but in the four years that I’ve lived in Clarksville, I’ve only had one neighbor stay the same. You have people stationed here for a year, 18 months, six months. The doors I knocked in ’24 who supported me, those people aren’t there anymore. It’s different people. A lot of the people that are here don’t vote here, they vote back home or they don’t register to vote at all because they move so much. Clarksville has a university, Austin Peay State University, that’s not in my district; it's in the district next to mine. But there are college students that live in my district, so it’s a double-edged sword. We love the college and we love the military, but it makes it really difficult to navigate the demographic of the voters because they change so often.

What do you think has most prepared you to hold your current office?

I think it’s the experiences that I’ve been through. I grew up in a lower-income family in the small town of Ashland City in Cheatham County. My dad was a construction worker, my mom went to college to become a paralegal, and we didn’t have a lot of money. Growing up, I was taught, You work for the things you want, period. You don’t ask for handouts. Then I went to school to get a bachelor’s degree in multimedia journalism.

Growing up and watching my parents struggle, and then becoming a mother myself and seeing how difficult it is just to be able to afford formula, food, diapers, toys… Everything my daughter had was handed down to us or found on Facebook Marketplace. The older I got, the more I learned that a lot of this is policy. Our General Assembly has refused to cut the grocery tax. We’re one of 13 states that still has one, and it’s at nine and a half percent. We’re paying more for what? People shouldn’t have to struggle to get access to a healthcare professional to get their medication. That’s a policy decision. They could expand Medicaid, but they won’t. Our roads shouldn’t have potholes every quarter-foot or have you sitting in traffic for three hours a day. We could invest in mass transit, but they won’t. I was a single mom for the first three years of my daughter’s life. I was working three jobs while going to college full-time. So knowing and seeing and living the struggle helps me understand the struggle. I’m not a single mom anymore, but we still struggle. If you don’t live this, you don’t understand it. This is nothing against my opponent [Lankford], but we’re both 31 and he’s not married, he doesn’t have any kids, and he lives on property owned by his family.

Completely. He’s a senior banker or something like that, but has he had to serve tables before and rely on tips to pay his bills? He’s never had to worry about getting his children to daycare so he can get to work, and he’s never had to figure out, Well, my kids are sick, so they can’t go to daycare, and now I’ve got to call out of work. My lived experiences are what made me feel qualified for this, because to be a representative is to represent the people, and I grew up seeing what hard work looks like.

What other races were you excited about or watching closely last Tuesday?

The top three races in Tennessee that we were looking to flip were mine, Jesse Huseth down in Memphis, and Brian Goldberg out in Knoxville. I was really curious to know if they were winning voters, and they did. They flipped their seats, and we flipped three additional seats that we weren’t really honed in on. I was disappointed that Jerri Green didn’t win against Marsha Blackburn, but I’m extremely proud of her for how close she got and the campaign that she ran. I was also disappointed in the congressional races that we were looking at with Justin Pearson, Chaz Mulder, and Darden Copeland, but I was very proud of Victoria Broderick for flipping her seat. That’s why you don’t underestimate women; we just show up out of nowhere sometimes! All in all, I’m fairly happy. We have 11 new Democrats going into the House, and I think the chairman said this is the biggest freshman class since either the ’80s or the ’90s. It’s really exciting for Democrats in the state and hopefully gives people more hope that we will continue moving forward.

What’s first on your docket, legislation-wise?

Well, priority number one is my district, always. But as far as legislation, I mentioned Miley’s Law. Outside of reproductive access, I ran my campaign on affordability for working families, so I would love to carry a bill about cutting the tax on diapers and potentially formula. If we can cut the grocery tax, the formula tax goes away, because formula is taxed with our food percentage. I want to cut property taxes for veterans—they served our country, give them a break—and then there was one about creating a lottery for a Tennessee fund for veterans, specifically, that would give them additional help with finding things like healthcare access, mental healthcare, housing, jobs, whatever it may be. My district is so military-heavy that supporting them is so important to me. I plan to be a strong advocate against school vouchers; I am wholeheartedly for fully funding our public schools. I want to make sure our schools are fully funded and that teachers are paid what they’re worth and kids are eating in school, and that we’re not funding private entities with public dollars.

Last question: How are you celebrating your big win and new role?

There really hasn’t been any opportunity to celebrate. I’m still trying to respond to text messages from election night. [Laughs.] I was put on payroll at midnight after the election, so it’s been go-go-go since, but I can tell you that I’m able to sleep better knowing that I’m not stressed out so much by the election. My oldest daughter, who’s nine, was missing me a lot during the campaign, so she's like, “Do you actually get to be home now?” I was like, “Uh, kind of.” She’s my biggest supporter.

This interview has been edited and condensed.

Maple Pumpkin Overnight Oats

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

“I don’t have time” should never be an excuse to skip breakfast. Take this overnight oat-chia-pudding hybrid, which comes together in barely 10 minutes. (I’m betting that’s less time than it takes to stand in line at the grocery store to buy a container of worse-tasting, more expensive oatmeal!) Grab a can of pumpkin and some baking staples, mix it up, then pop the bowl in the fridge. The next morning you’ll be rewarded with a lightly sweet, mousse-like treat that’s actually good for you—don’t forget to finish each bowl with a dollop of yogurt for a pumpkin-pie-with-whipped-cream vibe. Self-care rarely tastes this good.

Using an unsweetened soy or nut milk will keep the pudding more pumpkin-forward than dairy milk (and keeps the recipe vegan, of course), but any milk you have on hand will work here.

Healthy cooking doesn’t have to be complicated. In fact, often the simpler it is, the better it tastes. In Good for You, Test Kitchen editor Rebecca Firkser shares nourishing, craveable recipes that you can pull off in 10 ingredients or fewer.

Whisk one 15-oz. can pure pumpkin purée, 1½ cups unsweetened milk of choice, 2 tsp. vanilla bean paste or vanilla extract, ¼ tsp. Diamond Crystal or Morton kosher salt, ¼ cup pure maple syrup, and ½ tsp. ground cinnamon or cardamom in a large bowl until smooth. Add 3 Tbsp. chia seeds and whisk to combine. Let sit 10 minutes. (Doing this gives the seeds a head start on hydrating and prevents them from sticking together. But if you can’t spare the time, just give the mixture a good stir before serving.)

Stir chia mixture again, then add 1¼ cups old-fashioned oats and mix well to combine. Cover oat mixture and chill at least 8 hours.

Spoon oat mixture into bowls and top with plain whole-milk yogurt, unsalted roasted pumpkin seeds (pepitas), and/or granola, maple syrup, and cinnamon as desired.

Do Ahead: Oat mixture (without toppings) can be made 4 days ahead. Keep chilled.

Too Busy for Breakfast? Make These Pumpkin Overnight Oats

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Healthy cooking doesn’t have to be complicated. In fact, often the simpler it is, the better it tastes. In Good for You, Test Kitchen editor Rebecca Firkser shares feel-good recipes for any day of the week.

I’m not going to tell you that breakfast is the most important meal of the day. Humans have been on the planet for hundreds of thousands of years and our ability to thrive simply isn’t rooted in whether we eat first thing in the morning. In fact, the cliché is barely a century old, stemming not from nutritional science but good old-fashioned advertising.

Throughout the 19th and 20th centuries, advocates for breakfast cereal used religious rhetoric and maternal guilt to fuel their marketing efforts, while public relations executives at a pork company convinced doctors to recommend a heartier breakfast of bacon and eggs for sustained energy, aimed at working families.

Yet today breakfast is one of the most commonly skipped meals. So instead of wagging my finger at you about missing something so “important,” I think it’s worth asking: Why did you miss it in the first place?

On a jam-packed holiday like Thanksgiving, there are plenty of reasons. You have a turkey to roast. The table has to be set. Someone needs to pick up the wine. And oh, there’s still the pie dough to roll out! Maybe you even want to “save room” for the glorious feast you’re putting so much effort into.

But skipping this meal does come at a cost. Even if you aren’t ravenous, it’s metabolically valuable to eat in the morning. Breakfast stabilizes energy, and studies have shown that forgoing it leads to a reduced consumption of important nutrients like fiber, potassium, iron, and calcium, often found in morning staples like fruit, whole grains, and dairy. Without some food to start the day, you risk an energy dip, brain fog, and downright hanger. Which, let’s be honest, we don’t need in a kitchen overflowing with dirty dishes and extended relatives.

I have an easy solution. Making breakfast on Thanksgiving (or any day, really) doesn’t have to be a last-minute hassle. It can be quick to assemble and still feel special—like you’re really taking care of yourself, because you are. Take this overnight oats–chia pudding hybrid, which comes together in barely 10 minutes the night before you plan to eat it.

Open up a can of pumpkin purée—odds are you bought extras that didn’t become a pie. Mix it with oats, maple syrup, and whichever milk you have in the fridge. A scoop of chia seeds adds bonus nutrition and prevents the oats from turning gluey as they hydrate overnight.

The next morning you’ll be rewarded with a lightly sweet, mousse-like treat that’s actually good for you. It’ll keep you full and firing on all cylinders for a day of turkey roasting, potato mashing, and vibe managing. It may not be the most important meal of the day—at least on Thanksgiving—but it will be delicious.

Learn to love cottage cheese and hit your protein goals with this berry smoothie.

Turn bone broth into a complete and nourishing meal—all in 10 minutes.

Try these high-fiber breakfast recipes to get you, ahem, moving.

These 9 Hotels for Broadway Lovers Put You in the Heart of NYC’s Theater District

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

As a Californian who discovered the wonderful world of Broadway by borrowing CDs from my local library, it was only fitting that the first trip I ever planned by myself was to New York City—and the entire itinerary revolved around collecting Playbills. Now, after living in the area for more than two decades, I’m that unlikely New Yorker who still claims Times Square as her favorite and most-frequented neighborhood. I just love the Theater District—where else can you find 41 venues featuring the world's best live theater all within a single mile?

In this age of event tourism, Broadway has remained a constant. And seeing a show is about more than just the storyline of a play or the numbers in a musical—it’s an experience that you’re a part of. Over my 20 years as an entertainment editor and journalist, I’ve interviewed numerous Broadway stars, and they always say they feed off of, and are fueled by, audience reactions. You’re not just a passive viewer; your presence drives the show. It's something special to take part in.

If you've got a Broadway trip in your future, chances are you're going to need a hotel that will help you maximize your experience. The list below features properties with themed suites, theater lore, and locations just steps from the Great White Way. Read on for the best places to stay in NYC for a show-stopping Broadway getaway.

Closest theaters: 1-minute walk to Palace Theatre, 3-minute walk to James Earl Jones Theatre and Lunt-Fontanne TheatreWhy we love it: Right across from Palace Theatre, pre- and post-show prix-fixe menus, Studio 54 history

These days, Studio 54 is one of Broadway’s most popular stages (currently home to The Rocky Horror Show ), but back in 1977, Ian Schrager opened it as a nightclub, and it has since come to epitomize celebrity culture for a generation. Schrager brought that same visionary spirit when he opened the 452-room Times Square Edition in 2019, just across the street from the Palace Theatre. One of my favorite spots in Times Square is the hotel's Terrace and Outdoor Garden, a lush dining oasis that offers three-course Broadway Express prix-fixe menus both pre- and post-show. For those looking for an upgraded theater experience, the Edition's Showstopping Stay package—which can be booked up to 10 days in advance—includes two premium tickets to select shows, dinner with Champagne, theater-themed amenities, and house-made snacks.

Closest theaters: 9-minute walk to Studio 54, 10-minute walk to Vivian Beaumont Theater, 11-minute walk to Broadway TheatreWhy we love it: Central Park views, Broadway VIP Experience

Though a few blocks north of the Theater District thrills, the Mandarin Oriental is still less than a 15-minute walk to most theaters—yet it feels a world apart. It overlooks Central Park in one direction, and its 75-foot lap pool has views of the Hudson River. Guests are pampered with a true five-star experience every step of the way, whether it’s raising a glass at The Bar speakeasy or lounging around at celeb-favorite MO Lounge. You can also rub shoulders with the stars via the hotel's Broadway VIP Experience package, which includes an hour-long meet-and-greet with a Broadway cast member over bites and Champagne at the lounge. Also included are orchestra tickets (choose from 13 shows, including Hamilton and Harry Potter and the Cursed Child) and show merch, as well as add-ons like a theater transfer in a Mercedes, a 90-minute guided tour of the Museum of Broadway, or even acting lessons or a private concert with a stage star.

Closest theaters: 3-minute walk to Belasco Theatre, 4-minute walk to Hudson Theatre, 5-minute walk to Stephen Sondheim TheatreWhy we love it: Official partner of the Tony Awards, recently refreshed, early check-in option

If hotels could earn Tonys, the Sofitel New York would be a clear winner. As a longtime partner of Broadway’s iconic awards show, many a theater star has walked through its doors. That includes George Clooney during his Broadway debut in Good Night, and Good Luck, Jonathan Groff during his turn as Bobby Darin in Just in Time, and Mia Farrow while she was in The Roommate. Just last year, the property underwent a full makeover, reimagining its 398 rooms and suites (some with Empire State Building views), as well as its lobby, fitness center, and dining options (including Social 45 Bar & Bistro), drawing on the Art Deco glamour of Paris and New York. While an exclusive Broadway package is currently in the works, the Sofitel does currently offer The Ultimate Manhattan Luxury Staycation experience, which includes a welcome drink, dining credit, signature pastries, and early check-in at noon, perfect for squeezing in an extra Wednesday or weekend matinee.

Closest theaters: 2-minute walk to Eugene O’Neill Theatre, Walter Kerr, and Longacre TheatreWhy we love it: Quiet, designed by a Tony-winning scenic designer, curated theater mementos

Despite being footsteps away from several Broadway theaters, Civilian Hotel manages to create a cozy boutique experience, starting with an entrance just around the corner from Eighth Avenue. One step inside, and you feel like you’ve earned a backstage pass— hotelier Jason Pomeranc teamed up with scenic designer David Rockwell (Hairspray, Legally Blonde, Elf) on a classic theater theme featuring everything from marquee-inspired lighting to dramatic curtains. Even its 203 guest rooms channel vintage dressing room vibes. Rockwell's goal was to create a space for NYC’s creatives—performers, designers, directors, producers, and writers—that paid homage to classic Broadway. Stop by the Blue Vault tasting room and lounge to spot some theater relics, and the Olio Collection rooftop bar for more than 250 pieces of theater-inspired art.

Closest theaters: 1-minute walk to Palace Theatre, 3-minute walk to Marquis TheatreWhy we love it: Location across from TKTS booth, buzzy Sky Lobby

When it comes to Theater District locations, you can't find much better than the 661-room Tempo by Hilton New York Times Square. Making its debut in the dense area in August 2023, it snagged a prime spot right across from Duffy Square’s TKTS booth, the bright red discount ticket outlet that sells last-minute show tickets for up to 50% off (psst, check here for today’s offers). Even closer is the Palace Theatre, currently home to the musical The Lost Boys, which the property has partnered with on a themed suite, bookable through September 14, 2027. Inside the suite, you'll find an ’80s seaside feel mimicking the musical’s world of Santa Carla.

Closest theaters: 2-minute walk to Eugene O’Neill Theatre, Walter Kerr, and Ambassador Theatre; half a dozen more a 3-minute walk awayWhy we love it: Vacation vibes at its Bar Sprezzatura, daily complimentary wine in the Living Room Lobby during Evening Social Hour, Roundabout Theatre Company partnership

The first time I stepped off the elevator into Kimpton Theta New York’s Bar Sprezzatura, I immediately craved an Aperol spritz. That’s how much I was transported to the Italian Riviera. The contrast between the lively Midtown streets below (the bar sits on the 15th floor) and the laid-back rooftop bar made me fall in love with the 364-key property instantly, and with multiple two- and three-bedroom suites, it's perfect for families and groups looking to book a foodie-friendly Broadway bonding trip. This November, the hotel launched the Roundabout Theatre Co. x Kimpton Theta package, giving guests 20% off tickets to Roundabout Theatre Co. shows (currently The Rocky Horror Show and Imaginary Invalid) plus pre- or post-show drinks at the bar.

Closest theaters: Attached to Marquis Theatre, 1-minute walk to Minskoff Theatre, 2-minute walk to Richard Rodgers TheatreWhy we love it: Ideal for Marquis Theatre shows, home to NYC’s only rotating restaurant, Broadway Bound Dining Package

For a mega-sized hotel smack dab in the middle of Times Square, the Marriott Marquis still has a sense of character. When I first moved to the area 23 years ago, I used to frequent its eight-floor Broadway Lounge, looking out at Times Square and marveling that I had finally “made it." Nowadays, I still pop in for a dose of nostalgia, gazing up at the 36-floor atrium’s elevators leading to the hotel's 1,900 rooms—a constant in a world that’s changed so much since it opened in 1985. But that throwback feel comes with a new spring in its step, most notably at its 48th-floor spinning restaurant, The View, reimagined by Danny Meyer in 2024. (Note: TodayTix members get free bubbly and bar snacks here, depending on their tier.) But what makes the property so unique for theater fans is that it’s the only hotel ever built with a Broadway theater inside—yes, the Marquis Theatre is literally on the third floor. Also special: The Broadway Bound Dining Package includes the chance to mingle with a theater star!

Closest theaters: 2-minute walk to the Nederlander Theatre, New Amsterdam Theatre, and Lyric TheatreWhy we love it: Themed rooms, located on 42nd Street, close to other attractions

Now here’s a hotel that reflects both “A Whole New World” and “The Circle of Life.” Through its partnership with Disney’s two long-running musicals, Hilton New York Times Square guests can go all in with themed rooms curated by Broadway designer Devin Sparkles Design. Aladdin's Times Square Palace Suite is decked out just like Agrabah, down to a genie lamp, Moroccan magic carpets, and Jasmine-inspired silks. Over in The Lion King’s Royal Suite, it’s all about the wonders of the Serengeti Pridelands, with hand-painted Rafiki-like patterns and bird puppets flying from the ceiling. Both include wake-up calls from each musical’s most iconic characters—the Genie and Rafiki—as well as themed drinks. But perhaps the most valuable part of the package, especially for those driving in? Parking in Times Square.

Closest theaters: 1-minute walk to Hudson Theatre, Belasco Theatre, and Lyceum Theatre

Why we love it: Passageway running between Hudson Theatre’s entrance and stage door, June 2026 refresh

Ever since my first Broadway trip to NYC in 2002, I’ve been stage-dooring shows. The Hudson Theatre shows can be tricky for this, as the theater’s entrance is on 44th Street, but its stage door is on 45th. It wasn’t until I discovered that the Millennium Hotel had a secret passageway—which is also the entrance to the hotel—that I started snagging spots close enough to get autographs (including from Daniel Radcliffe during Merrily We Roll Along). That said, I've always been jealous of those actually staying at the 751-room property, with a trio of theaters literally at their doorstep. Those eager to dive even deeper into the Theater District's history will love that the property is just across the street from the Museum of Broadway.

Is it expensive to see a show on Broadway?

Yes, price tags can be high—the year-to-date average per ticket for 2026 is $122.67. But there are also plenty of ways to find discounts through lotteries, TodayTix, Theatr, and the TKTS Booth. I’ve only paid more than $100 five times in my life—and never more than $200. (That’s why I haven’t been to a movie in nearly a decade, but have seen nearly 40 shows in the last year!)

What show should I see on Broadway this winter?

For last-minute trips, hot tickets right now include Bowen Yang’s record-breaking run in Cole Escola’s Oh, Mary! (Lyceum Theatre), plus A Few Good Men (Vivian Beaumont Theatre), Much Ado About Nothing (Winter Garden), Other Desert Cities (Hudson Theatre), and School Girls; Or, The African Mean Girls Play (Samuel J. Friedman). And of course there are long-running hits like Wicked (Gershwin Theatre), Hamilton (Richard Rodgers Theatre), Harry Potter and the Cursed Child (Lyric Theatre), The Great Gatsby (Broadway Theatre), and Disney’s The Lion King (Minskoff Theatre).

Are there any shows I should plan a 2027 trip around?

As we’re standing on the precipice of one of the most anticipated Broadway seasons, there’s no better time to plan a theater getaway. Coming soon are Dolly: A True Original Musical (opening December 7 at St. James Theatre), the Julia Roberts and Paul Rudd–led Small Mouth Sounds (February 10 at Ethel Barrymore Theatre), Jonathan Groff’s The Amish Project (February 10 at Broadhurst Theatre), Dirty Dancing (February 12 at Lena Thorne Theatre), Evita on Broadway (February 27 at Winter Garden Theatre), Lin-Manuel Miranda’s Warriors (March 9 at Lunt-Fontanne Theatre), Purple Rain (March 12 at Majestic Theatre), The Sound of Music (March 23 at Vivian Beaumont Theatre), Damn Yankees (March 24 at Marquis Theatre), and the one I’m most thrilled about, the West End transfer Paddington: The Musical (March 30 at Al Hirschfield Theatre)—I already have my ticket for the first preview!

Every hotel review on this list has been written by a Condé Nast Traveler journalist who knows the destination and has visited that property. When choosing hotels, our editors consider properties across price points that offer an authentic and insider experience of a destination, keeping design, location, service, and sustainability credentials top of mind.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Artists Helen Marden and the Late Brice Marden Refashioned a Decommissioned 19th-Century Ferry Terminal

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Over the course of their more than 50-year marriage, artist Helen Marden and her late husband, the eminent painter Brice Marden, amassed an international collection of seriously eccentric—and deeply magical—houses, gardens, and studios, each designed around the double helix of art and family. They bought their first house, a cliffside cottage on the Greek island of Hydra, in 1973, and since then real estate and art have been entwined in their life, art facilitating the purchase of property, the properties both catalyst and carapace for the evolution of their art. An Italianate villa in upstate New York, a pair of traditional riads in Marrakech, a hotel set among the ruins of a 19th-century sugar plantation in the Caribbean, a second house by the port in Hydra, a Renaissance Revival town house in Manhattan, a stately manse on a sprawling estate in Pennsylvania—every one of their idiosyncratic properties has its own fascinating history, reflected in its interior. The homes are all documented in the upcoming book Brice & Helen Marden: Houses, Gardens, Studios (Gagosian), edited by this writer, with photography by Stephen Kent Johnson.

Helen Marden in the living room. Chair and side table by the Campana Brothers. The chimney is clad in Clé Tile. A watercolor by Henri Michaux rests on the mantel.

The living room includes chairs by Marcel Wanders (far left) and the Campana Brothers. Sculpture at right by Matthew Wang.

In the dining room, a Paavo Tynell pendant hangs above a rustic farm table with chairs by Jonathan Olivares and Maarten Baas. Amazonia marble covers one wall.

The Mardens were instinctively drawn to residences with endurance, places that have seen a few things, not newly built pleasure domes devoid of texture and soul. Swan House, the riparian hideaway in the middle of the Hudson River, epitomizes the couple’s embrace of the strange and the enchanted. Accessed by a long, narrow causeway that leads off the shore of Saugerties, the structure was built in 1877 as part of Long Dock, a ship terminal and moorage for large vessels conveying people and goods the length of the Hudson. It was also a landing for ferries crossing the river between Saugerties and the village of Tivoli. One can see Swan House shimmering in the landscape from the edge of Rose Hill, the Marden family’s 19th-century Tivoli estate.

The Depression, along with the advent of more efficient modes of transport, scuppered the last of the large ships landing at Long Dock in the early 1930s, but ferries continued to make the two-minute trip across the water until 1938. After that, the structure was decommissioned from professional service and sold, becoming the only privately owned peninsula on the Hudson. The Mardens purchased the property in 2011, but it took a full decade before they engaged designer Daniel Sachs and architect Kevin Lindores of the AD100 firm Sachs Lindores Architecture, Interiors to execute a full-scale renovation.

The primary bath features custom zellige tiles, a vintage Vistosi chandelier, an antique marble sink, and a tub by The Water Monopoly.

Sachs Lindores remodeled the entry with a windscreen of glass brick. The chair is by Gaetano Pesce.

Yellow zellige tiles meet a pale pink Aga range beneath a custom zinc hood in the kitchen. The pendant light is by Adolf Loos from WOKA.

Brice Marden’s For Helen – Valentine 2019 (2015–18) hangs on the black-tiled fireplace tower that mediates the bedroom and living room.

A custom bed is flanked by an antique French console table and a Charles Rohlfs hall chair. Lighting includes a Franz West chandelier and a floor lamp by Achille and Pier Giacomo Castiglioni.

“Early on we demolished a few walls to open up the views, but it was pretty raw. We’d just go over and watch the boats go by,” Helen recalls. “You have a completely different relationship with the river when you’re sitting in the middle of it.”

Given the famously cultivated, idiosyncratic tastes of the homeowners, the collaboration between Sachs Lindores and their clients was unusually fertile. Together, they reimagined the derelict building as a trick box for trapping the natural light that bounces off the water on all sides, with reflective, lead-coated copper siding and a zinc roof on the exterior and fields of luminous plaster, tile, and marble within. “Helen never shies away from a bold gesture. At first, she told us to paint the house red, but when we presented the idea of a glistening metal-clad box with light dancing on the skin, she immediately went for it,” Sachs recalls. “Brice and Helen were very interconnected in their aesthetic tastes and decision making,” Lindores adds. “As you might imagine, they studied every paint color and trim detail.”

Ruins of the 19th-century dock remain alongside the house.

A guest room is outfitted with a Pierre Jeanneret daybed, lounge chairs by Monika Mulder for IKEA, a vintage Kevin Walz standing lamp, and a Mathieu Matégot magazine rack. Painting by Helen Marden.

Sigmar Polke’s Untitled (1998) hangs above a vintage Walter Knoll sofa in the first-floor sitting room.

While retaining the shape of its original architectural envelope, the house was essentially rebuilt, with new window configurations, open circulation, and an exposed ceiling, all in service of maximizing watery vistas and ease of use. On the lofty upper level, the existing chimney, newly revealed in its full floor-to-ceiling height, was clad in glossy black brick tile. The monolithic form now feels like a high altar in a temple of light. Custom zellige tiles imported from Fez cover the walls of the kitchen and baths, where Helen’s genius for juxtaposing colors and materials comes into high relief.

The polyglot ensemble of furnishings hews closely to the signature Marden mix: Moroccan rugs; bold contemporary designs by the likes of Gaetano Pesce, Marcel Wanders, and the Campana Brothers; humble American folk pieces; 20th-century classics by Paavo Tynell, Adolf Loos, and Pierre Jeanneret; and a few Continental antiques to leaven the brew. “I just pick things that I like, things that make me happy,” Helen says, pooh-poohing the idea of some grand decorative vision. Her designers would demur. “She has an incredible eye,” Sachs insists. “The more crazy Helen would get, the more Brice liked it.”

Dua Lipa Talks About Her Book Club, and Her Favorite Authors Join Her for a Photo

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

It’s the 41st week of this year, and Dua Lipa has already read 22 books. Next up is Sufiyaan Salam’s novel, Wimmy Road Boyz, a coming of age story about a British Pakistani friend group. But fresh on her mind is Careless People: A Cautionary Tale of Power, Greed, and Lost Idealism by Sarah Wynn-Williams, which Lipa has just finished, logging the accomplishment in the Notes app on her orange iPhone.

“Do you know about it?” she asks me, eager to recommend the whistleblower’s memoir. “She’s getting censored because she started working at Facebook very early on and she dishes it all out.”

PAGE TURNERS Lipa at Albertine Books in October with seven of her hand-selected Service95 book club monthly reads. Shirt by Gucci; jewelry by Bulgari.

It should come as no surprise that Lipa, the internet’s favorite hardworking “vacanza queen,” gets most of her reading done while traveling. “I can read in cars. I don’t get sick. It’s kind of a flex,” she says. When we meet in New York in early October, Lipa has just returned from a trip to Madrid, where she performed with Shakira, doing what she humbly refers to as her “day job,” and Paris, for a Chanel show. No matter the destination, for work or play, Lipa will always toss a book—no e-readers—in her bag. “I love the feeling of a book. I love when all the pages get all fucked up a little bit,” she says.

Dressed in an off-duty uniform of blue jeans, a black Chanel sweater, leather boots, and a black Gucci purse big enough to stash a hardcover or two, Lipa, the 31-year-old British Albanian singer who has emerged as a leading literacy advocate, is gearing up for the London Literature Festival, which she guest-curated this year.

Her reading habit is impressive. Not just because she is a jet-set Grammy Award–winning pop star and half of a newlywed power couple (she and husband Callum Turner famously bonded over finishing the same chapter of Hernan Diaz’s novel, Trust) whose Instagram suggests that she exists in a perpetual state of glamour, but because, according to a recent statistic, more than half of American adults read approximately zero books in a year. “Sad,” Lipa says when I give her this stat.

Since 2021, Lipa has been using her spare time to run Service95—a website, newsletter, book club, and podcast—described as a “cultural concierge.” Through the platform, Lipa offers bespoke recommendations and publishes writing on a wide range of topics from style, travel, and wellness to politics and activism. It’s also where, since Service95’s inception, she has hosted in-depth conversations with authors from around the world and across genres. With dozens of podcast episodes under her belt so far, she’s quickly learned what it’s like to be on the other side of an interview. “I’ve done so many interviews myself and I’ve had to answer the same question over and over again. I’m quite sensitive to the fact that I don’t want to put the author in the same position of having to repeat themselves,” she says. “It’s one of my favorite things that I do. I feel like I get so much out of the conversations.”

Today, at the Villa Albertine, a French cultural institution on the Upper East Side, she’s brought her bookshelf to life, gathering seven of her favorite writers—memoirists, novelists, journalists, a National Book Award and Booker Prize winner—for a class photo. Standing together in one room, beneath a mural of a constellation, the stars align: Douglas Stuart, Brit Bennett, Patti Smith, Roxane Gay, Jennifer Clement, Patrick Radden Keefe, Min Jin Lee, and Lipa, all looking like the English department of your wildest dreams. Sitting next to Smith, the author of Just Kids, Lipa, one of the biggest celebrities in the world, appears to be starstruck. In a rare role reversal, Lipa is the fangirl, whipping out her phone to commemorate the occasion with a group selfie.

Here, her impact in the literary world is undeniable—and so is her range.

Between takes, Smith inquires about how many languages Lipa speaks—English, Albanian, some Spanish, and she’s learning French and Italian, she says. The Just Kids author quips that she only knows how to ask where the “biblioteca” is. “That’s kind of all you need,” jokes Keefe, author of Say Nothing, who tells me he often encounters young readers who were introduced to his work via Service95.

READ THE ROOM Dua Lipa and authors take a selfie at Albertine Books. Lipa’s shoes by Prada; jewelry by Bulgari.

Watching all of these literary titans in conversation with each other, it’s impressive to see them all embrace Lipa as an advocate of their work. “I’m sure that there are lots of other things she could be doing with her time that would be far more profitable than championing books,” says Bennett, author of The Vanishing Half. “It’s amazing that she does this out of a genuine love for reading.”

“It’s fun work. It’s good work,” insists Lipa, who is happy to disprove naysayers who think she just “holidays all the time.” “I’m having a good time, and I get all my work done, and I do what I want to do, and then I go on holiday, and I’m happy with that.” It’s what keeps her motivated: “Sometimes being underestimated gives me extra fire in my belly.”

In 2023, when Lipa launched her book club, which has racked up nearly 1 million Instagram followers, she joined the likes of Oprah Winfrey, Reese Witherspoon, Kaia Gerber, Dakota Johnson, Emma Roberts, and Jenna Bush Hager. But the saturation of the celebrity book club market gave her no hesitation. “If people feel a little bit inclined to just go and pick up a book and read something, we’re all doing our job,” she says. “I don’t care if someone’s going to go to this book club or the next one. As long as they’re reading, I’m happy.”

But Clement, author of Widow Basquiat, considers Service95 to be in a league of its own. “It is not simply curating individual titles; it is curating perspective,” she says. “Almost as if, taken together, the books themselves are forming an arc, a larger story unfolding through the choices.”

Lipa works closely with her book club director, Maria Padget, to select her monthly reads, guided not by what’s trending but instead, as Lee, the author of Pachinko who calls Lipa an “exemplary literary citizen,” puts it, by honoring “her intellectual curiosity,” often highlighting marginalized voices, and not absorbing literature through a strictly Western lens.

“When she looks at her readers, she doesn’t think what would be easiest for you to enjoy, or what would comfort you or challenge you the least,” says Stuart, author of Shuggie Bain. “Instead she signals, go on, you can read this, you should read this; read this challenging book, read this banned book, and see what they want to deny you, see what you can achieve and have access to what would make you more powerful!”

In the face of a burgeoning literacy crisis, global book bans, rampant censorship, and the rise of artificial intelligence, Service95 is a venue for Lipa to educate herself and her readers on global issues—encouraging an open dialogue and discourse. “I don’t shy away from it. I also like the fact that I do it on my own terms,” says Lipa, who calls activism an “important pillar” of Service95.

As a result, she’s spoken to Margaret Atwood about the fight for female reproductive rights and discussed sexual violence and intersectional feminism with Gay, author of Bad Feminist. “The book had already been out for more than a decade, so it was quite a thrill to see her introducing the work to a new audience,” the essayist says. Lipa has also pondered the impacts of AI with Nobel Prize laureate Kazuo Ishiguro and provided required reading on Israel and the war in Gaza. She’s even joined a reading group at a women’s prison in Surrey and opened the Manifesto Library—stocking banned books inside the Livraria Lello in Portugal.

“The whole goal was to get people to take a breather, learn about the thing that’s happening, and not to be afraid to ask questions or not be right all the time,” she says.

Lipa, who admits to using the Opal app, which limits her to an hour a day on social media to post her famous photo dumps, is hopeful Service95 can also serve as an antidote to doomscrolling and AI. “Not only are we declining in reading and not picking up a book, but the fact that you can have these cheat codes that completely bypass the whole point of the learning or the ingesting of information,” she says. “It’s just going to make us stupider as people.”

She credits her mother, Anesa, for her love of reading, reminiscing on their weekends spent at a London bookshop. “I basically would just sit there and read for hours and hours and hours. I loved it,” she says, rattling off the names of authors from her childhood, like Jacqueline Wilson and Malorie Blackman and the Mr. Men and Mary-Kate and Ashley Olsen series. Reading is formative to who she is as an artist; it makes her a better songwriter, as she tells me, but it is also fundamental to who she is as a person. “I feel like we get a better understanding of ourselves, of our friends, of our family members, of the people around us, what they might be going through, what’s happening in their life by just picking up a book,” says Lipa.

When I bring up the recent claim from influencer Alix Earle that she had never read a book (apart from maybe Dr. Seuss), Lipa considers the topic thoughtfully and speaks without passing any judgment, generously suggesting the possibility that maybe, as a public figure, Earle said this as a protective measure, to shield her from further prodding and criticism. “Books are so personal, I think people also get intimidated,” she says. “Books should just be enjoyable. No one’s expecting anything from the person reading it. You don’t have to overanalyze it. It’s just supposed to make you feel something.”

And true to form, she offers a book recommendation: “I love The Girls by Emma Cline. I think that’s a cool gateway drug book.”

For Lipa, it all harkens back to searching for that feeling. She returns to her recent interview with Ishiguro, the Klara and the Sun author, who drew parallels between pop music and literature and their ability to foster connection. “He said the heart has so many rooms, so many things that make us, and AI can’t do that,” she says. “There is something really encouraging about that, that some things can't be replicated. Our real humanness and the love we feel, the loneliness even, it can make us feel even more alive and more human.” Perhaps Lipa’s book club is an act of resistance, but it’s also, to borrow a line from her last album, an act of radical optimism.

Fashion Editors: Lorenzo Posocco (Lipa), Kia D. Goosby (Bennett, Clement, Gay, Keefe, Stuart).

BNY taps Figment to support staking on its digital asset custody platform

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Eligible asset managers, ETF issuers, and other institutional clients will be able to stake supported assets held with BNY.

Banking giant BNY will use Figment's validator infrastructure to support its staking offering, allowing eligible institutions to stake assets through the bank's existing Digital Asset Custody service.

Asset managers, exchange-traded fund issuers, corporates, and wealth management firms are among the staking clients BNY will serve, Figment said Thursday. Eligible clients will be able to earn staking rewards on supported assets held on the platform. A Figment spokesperson told The Block that ETH will initially be supported, followed by SOL. The companies did not specify a launch date.

"As institutional participation in staking grows, the focus is shifting from simply accessing staking to how it is delivered," BNY Head of Digital Asset Custody Ralf Roth said in a statement. "Offering staking with Figment in a trusted custody framework can give institutions greater confidence in how they participate, while supporting the standards of security, oversight, and accountability they expect."

BNY launched its digital asset custody service in October 2022, initially accepting bitcoin and ether from select clients. Last month, BNY and Ripple expanded their custody agreement to cover XRP and RLUSD and also announced plans for BNY clients to mint and redeem RLUSD through the bank.

Coinbase Prime first integrated Figment's Ethereum Ethereum (ETH) staking service in early 2024. The partnership had enabled more than $2 billion in staked assets by October 2025, the firms said when they announced support for more networks, including Solana Solana (SOL), Cardano, and Sui.

"Figment is bringing its validator infrastructure and staking expertise to BNY's Digital Asset Custody platform, giving institutional clients another way to participate in staking using their existing platform and servicing workflows," Figment Head of Revenue Josh Deems said.

Figment says it provides staking services to more than 1,500 institutions, including asset managers, custodians, exchanges, and wallets.

This GTA 6 Clip Looks So Real, Fans Can't Believe It's In-Game Footage

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

If you're at all interested in GTA 6, you'll probably have seen this doing the rounds already. A video is going viral on social media, because it's fooling people into thinking it's real-life footage — but it's actually the game.

Originally posted by onlyindade on Instagram, the video has since been shared by the official Rockstar Games account, with many taking that as confirmation it's actually a video of Vice City within GTA 6.

The clip begins with a view from inside a moving vehicle as it drives past the Cortez Hotel, a Vice City location inspired by the real world Colony Hotel in Miami. The view flips half-way through, looking out onto the road.

We see several custom cars (the same ones that have been recreated in real life) as well as an impressive number of NPCs going about their business.

It's extremely convincing; the way the camera sways mimics the sort of videos captured on smartphones. You can actually see a direct comparison of the video against real life here:

Fans are getting really excited about this new clip as it really highlights the game's attention to detail and realism, with some still unconvinced it's actually GTA 6.
